On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs

De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus · Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377 · books 1-11

No complete published translation into English by a scholar that we know of. An English translation of books 1-5 by J. Wilkins is announced in the Cambridge Galen Translations. Books 6-8: a French translation with the Greek by C. Manco (doctoral thesis, Montpellier and Bologna, 2020). The prefaces of books 6-11 and the first two entries of book 6: English by M. Coughlin (ancientmedicine.org, 2023). All eleven books: a free French version by H. Fayard (1548). An unreviewed machine translation into English of Kühn XI and XII, the volumes containing these books, was posted at Source Library (sourcelibrary.org) during 2026. This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. The small grey numbers are the pages of Kühn's Greek edition in books 1-5 and the entry numbers (book.chapter.entry) in books 6-11. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks; those checks are automated agreement tests, not a reading of the Greek, so a passage without the rule is not checked either. Words in square brackets were supplied by the model for the sense; Greek in parentheses gives the original word; any remark about a doubtful or corrupt reading is the model's, not a scholar's.

Check (a): the English back-translated into Greek by a third model shares at least half of the unit's term lemmas with the source. Check (b): the two renderings agree on at least 70% of the unit's target terms. Check (c): a model judge, salted with 10 planted errors and 10 anchors it had to get right, finds at least 80% of the term renderings within the LSJ entry's senses, with at most 2 outside. A unit fails when it fails two of three.

Flags, under each passage: places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors. Sentence by sentence, each rendering is compared with the Greek for numbers, negations and words of more or less, and for the names of drugs and parts of the body (a name in the English with none in the Greek, or a name in the Greek with none in the English). These are word-matching tests: a flag often comes from a sentence pair that is aligned a little off, a paraphrase or a name the model kept in Greek, and a passage with no flag is not checked by them either. The flags play no part in the unit verdicts.

Greek (Kühn)
καὶ ταῦτα ποιοῦσιν οἱ πλείους αὐτῶν ἀμαθεῖς ὑπάρχοντες οὐ τῆς φυσικῆς θεωρίας 460 μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν λογικῶν μεθόδων, αἷς ἀναγκαῖον ἐστιν χρῆσθαι τὸν ὁτιοῦν ἀποδείξαντα, ὥστε μήθ’ ὅσα καλῶς εὕρηται τοῖς φυσικοῖς ἀνδράσιν μήθ’ ὑπὲρ ὧν αὐτῶν εὐλόγως ἠπόρηται γινώσκοντες, ἑκατέροις πολλάκις ἀποφαίνεσθαι τολμῶσι τἀναντία. φέρε δὲ ὦ πρὸς θεῶν, εἴπερ ἐγὼ νῦν ἀρξάμενος ὧδέ πως λέγειν, ὡς τεττάρων ὄντων στοιχείων ἀέρος καὶ γῆς ὕδατός τε καὶ πυρὸς, ὡς οὐδενὸς ἄλλου ξύμφυτόν ἐστι τὸ λευκὸν καὶ λαμπρὸν χρῶμα, πλὴν αὐγῆς καὶ πυρὸς, εἶτα καὶ τὸν Ἐμπεδοκλέα καὶ τοὺς ἄλλους τινας τῶν φυσικῶν ἐπὶ τῷ λόγῳ καλέσας μάρτυρας, ἅπαντα τὰ λαμπρὰ σώματα πυρὸς πλείστου μετέχειν ἀποφηναίην ἀμελήσας ἐμβλέψαι χιόνι καὶ ψιμυθίῳ καὶ κρυστάλλῳ ἑτέροις τε μυρίοις τῶν λαμπροτάτων τε ἅμα καὶ ψυχροτάτων, εἶτά τινος αὐτὰ προχειριζομένου, τὸν ἀπὸ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἔλεγχον ἀποδιδράσκων ἀξιοίην ἐπὶ τὸν λόγον ἰέναι καὶ τούτῳ σκοπεῖσθαι περὶ φύσεως πραγμάτων, οὐκ αἰσθήσεσιν ἀλόγοις ἐπιτρέπειν τὸ πᾶν, ἆρ’ οὐκ ἂν τοῖς νοῦν ἔχουσιν μαίνεσθαι δόξαιμι, μηδ’ ἀφ’ ὧν ἄρχομαι τοῦ λόγου γινώσκων;
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κρυστάλλῳ, ψιμυθίῳ, αὐγῆς
And they do this, most of them being ignorant not only of natural theory 460 but also of the logical methods which anyone who demonstrates anything whatsoever must necessarily employ, so that, knowing neither what has been well discovered by the natural philosophers nor the matters about which those same men have reasonably been in doubt, they often dare to declare the opposite of both. Come now, by the gods: if I, beginning now to speak somewhat as follows—that, there being four elements, air and earth and water and fire, the white and bright colour is inherent by nature in nothing else except radiance and fire—and then, having called Empedocles and certain others of the natural philosophers as witnesses to the argument, were to declare that all bright bodies partake of the greatest amount of fire, having neglected to look at snow and white lead and ice and countless other things that are at once the brightest and the coldest, and then, when someone brought these forward, were to run away from the refutation that comes from sense-perception and demand that one go to reason and by this investigate the matters concerning nature, and not entrust the whole to irrational senses—would I not seem to those who have intelligence to be mad, not even knowing the things from which I begin my argument?
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς τεττάρων ὄντων στοιχείων ... ὡς οὐδενὸς ἄλλου (repeated ὡς, anacoluthon), αὐγῆς, ψιμυθίῳ, μηδ’ ἀφ’ ὧν ἄρχομαι τοῦ λόγου γινώσκων
And most of them do this while being ignorant not only of natural theory 460 but also of the logical methods which anyone who demonstrates anything whatsoever must necessarily use; so that, knowing neither what has been rightly discovered by the natural philosophers nor the very points about which these men have reasonably been in doubt, they often dare to declare the opposite in both cases. Come now, by the gods: suppose I now began to speak in some such way as this: that, there being four elements, air and earth and water and fire, the white and bright colour is innate in nothing else except light and fire. Suppose that I then called Empedocles and certain others of the natural philosophers as witnesses to the argument, and declared that all bright bodies partake of fire in the greatest measure, having neglected to look at snow and white lead and ice and countless other things that are at once brightest and coldest. Suppose further that, when someone put these forward, I ran away from the refutation that comes from sensation and demanded that we proceed to reason, and by it examine the nature of things, rather than entrust everything to the irrational senses. Would I not seem to those who have sense to be mad, not knowing even the things from which I begin my argument?

Greek (Kühn)
ἐξ αἰσθήσεως 461 γὰρ, οἶμαι, καὶ δι’ αἰσθήσεως ἅπαντα τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν ἀξιωμάτων ἐμάθομεν, ὡς ὁ μὲν ἥλιος λαμπρὸς ἐστιν, αἱ δὲ φλόγες ὑπόξανθοι, τῶν δ’ ἀνθράκων οἱ πλεῖστοι ξανθοί. εἰ μὲν οὖν ἀποστητέον ἐστὶ ταῖς αἰσθήσεσιν, οὐδεμίαν ἕξομεν ἀπόδειξιν οὔθ’ ἡμεῖς ὧν νῦν εἰρήκαμεν οὔτε Ἐμπεδοκλῆς ὡδὶ γράφων, Ἠέλιον μὲν θερμὸν ὁρᾷν καὶ λαμπρὸν ἁπάντη. εἰ δέ ἐστιν ἀληθῆ ταῦτα, δῆλον ὡς πολὺ πρότερον αὐτῶν τὴν αἴσθησιν εἶναι χρὴ πιστήν. ἀλλ’ ἄρχεσθαι μέν φασιν ἀπὸ τῶν αἰσθήσεων ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν ἀνθρώπους γε ὄντας, οὐ μὴν ἐπ’ αὐτήν γε προσήκει καταμένειν διὰ παντὸς, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ τὸ θειότερον ἰέναι τὸν λόγον. αἰσθήσεως μὲν γὰρ καὶ τοῖς ἀλόγοις ζώοις μετεῖναι, λόγου δὲ μόνον τοῖς θεοῖς. κᾀντεῦθεν ἀρξάμενοι, δολιχὸν ἀποτείνουσι τὸν λόγον. καί τινες ἐξ αὐτῶν καὶ τὸν Ἀναξαγόραν ἐπικαλοῦνται μάρτυρα, περὶ τῆς χιόνος ἀποφηνάμενον, ὡς οὐκ εἴη λευκή.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀποστητέον
For it is from sensation, 461 I think, and through sensation that we learned all axioms of this kind, that the sun is bright, that flames are yellowish, and that most live coals are yellow. If, then, we must renounce the senses, we shall have no demonstration, neither we of what we have just said, nor Empedocles when he writes thus: "the sun, hot to behold and bright in every direction." But if these things are true, it is clear that sensation must be trustworthy long before them. But they say that it is indeed necessary, for beings who are at any rate human, to begin from the senses; yet it is not fitting to remain at sensation throughout, but to proceed to what is more divine, reason. For in sensation even the irrational animals have a share, but in reason only the gods. And starting from this point, they stretch out a long discourse. And some of them even call upon Anaxagoras as a witness, who declared concerning snow that it is not white.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λόγου δὲ μόνον τοῖς θεοῖς (literally 'reason only to the gods'; rendered 'we share ... reason only with the gods' to avoid denying reason to humans; possibly corrupt for καὶ τοῖς θεοῖς), ὁρᾷν (infinitive in the Empedocles verse, taken as imperative 'behold'), πολὺ πρότερον αὐτῶν (rendered 'long before them', i.e. prior to these propositions)
For it is from sensation 461, I think, and through sensation that we have learned all such axioms as these: that the sun is bright, that flames are yellowish, and that most live coals are yellow. If, then, we must abandon the senses, we shall have no demonstration, neither we of the things we have just stated, nor Empedocles when he writes thus: 'Behold the sun, hot and bright everywhere.' But if these things are true, it is clear that sensation must be trustworthy long before them. 'But,' they say, 'it is necessary for us, being human, to begin from the senses; yet it is not fitting to remain with sensation forever, but rather to proceed to what is more divine, reason. For we share sensation even with irrational animals, but reason only with the gods.' And beginning from this point they draw the argument out to great length. And some of them even call upon Anaxagoras as a witness, since he declared of snow that it was not white.

K11.461-462K. XI 461–462, ch. 2.1, 2.2 difficulty low (0.72/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
οὗτος ἄρα, φασί, φυσικὸς ἀνὴρ ὑπὲρ τὴν αἴσθησίν ἐστιν καὶ καταφρονεῖ 462 μὲν τῶν ταύτης φαντασμάτων, ἐπὶ δὲ τὸν λόγον ἀνέρχεται, καὶ τούτῳ τὴν τῶν ὄντων θηρᾶται φύσιν. ἐμὲ δ’ εἰ χρὴ τὸ παριστάμενον εἰπεῖν, ὡς ἐλευθέριόν τε καὶ παρ’ ὅλον τὸν βίον ἀλήθειαν σπουδάσαντα, μελαγχολίας ἐπέκεινα προεληλυθέναι νομίζω τοὺς τὰ τοιαῦτα ληροῦντας. εἰ μὲν γὰρ ἀνατρέψουσι τὰ διὰ τῶν αἰσθήσεων ἐναργῶς φαινόμενα, πόθεν ἄρξονται τῶν ἀποδείξεων οὐχ ἕξουσιν. εἰ δ’ ὡς ἀπὸ πιστῶν ἄρξονται, πῶς ὕστερον εὐλόγως ἀπιστήσουσιν; αἱ γὰρ τῶν ἀποδείξεων ἀρχαὶ πιστότεραι τῶν ἀποδεικνυμένων, ἃ τῆς ἐξ ἑτέρων δεῖται πίστεως. αἱ δ’ ἀρχαὶ τῶν ἀποδείξεων οὔ μόνον αὐταὶ καθ’ ἑαυτὰς, ἀλλὰ καὶ πρὸς τὴν τῶν ζητουμένων εὕρεσιν ὑπάρχουσι πισταί. Καὶ ἔγωγ’ ἂν εὐξαίμην ἅπαντα τὰ πράγματα ταῖς αἰσθήσεσιν ἡμῶν ἐναργῶς ὑποπίπτειν καὶ μηδὲν εἶναι τὸ διαφεῦγον αὐτὰς, ὡς οὕτως ἂν οὔτ’ ἄπορον οὔτ’ ἀμφισβητούμενον ἦν οὐδέν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
This man, then, they say, being a natural philosopher, stands above sense-perception and despises 462 its appearances, but ascends to reason, and by this hunts out the nature of the things that are. But if I must say what occurs to me, as a free man and one who throughout his whole life has been zealous for the truth, I think that those who babble such things have advanced beyond melancholy. For if they overturn the things that appear clearly through the senses, they will have no starting point from which to begin their demonstrations. But if they begin from them as from things trustworthy, how will they afterwards reasonably distrust them? For the principles of demonstrations are more trustworthy than the things demonstrated, which require credibility from other things. But the principles of demonstrations are trustworthy not only in themselves, by themselves, but also with a view to the discovery of the things under investigation. And I for my part would pray that all things fell clearly under our senses and that there were nothing that escaped them, since in that way nothing would be either insoluble or disputed.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐλευθέριόν, τὸ παριστάμενον
This man, then, they say, being a natural philosopher, is above the senses and despises 462 their appearances; he ascends instead to reason, and by it hunts down the nature of the things that are. But if I must say what occurs to me, as one of free and liberal spirit who has pursued truth throughout his whole life, I consider that those who talk such nonsense have gone beyond melancholy. For if they overturn what appears clearly through the senses, they will have no point from which to begin their demonstrations. But if they begin from these as from things worthy of trust, how will they afterwards reasonably withdraw their trust? For the starting-points of demonstrations are more trustworthy than the things demonstrated, which need to be made credible from other things. And the starting-points of demonstrations are trustworthy not only in and by themselves, but also for the discovery of the things sought. For my part, I would pray that all things fell clearly under our senses and that nothing escaped them, since in this way nothing would be either insoluble or disputed.

K11.462-463K. XI 462–463, ch. 2.2 difficulty low (1.19/100; thin 1, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ἐπεὶ δ’ ἐκφεύγει τινὰ, πειρατέον ἐπάγειν αὐτοῖς τὸν λόγον, ἀλλ’ οὔτε πόῤῥωθεν, ὡς ἐκεῖνοι ποιοῦσιν, οὔτ’ ἀπέραντα συλλογιζομένους, ἀλλὰ πρῶτον μὲν 463 τὰ σημαινόμενα τῶν ὀνομάτων ἀκριβῶς διαιρουμένους, ἵνα μή τις παρὰ τὴν ὁμωνυμίαν ἐν τοῖς μετὰ ταῦτα ἀπάτη γίγνηται. δεύτερον δὲ τὸ καθ’ αὑτό τι δρῶν, τοῦ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ἀφορίζοντας. εἴρηται δ’ ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν ὑπὲρ ἀμφοῖν ἀκριβῶς, καὶ τὰ σημαινόμενα τῶν δυνάμει θερμῶν, ἢ ψυχρῶν, ἢ ξηρῶν, ἢ ὑγρῶν, ἢ ὁτιοῦν ἄλλο λεγομένων εἶναι διῄρηται. καὶ ὅπως ἄν τις εὑρίσκοι τὴν οἰκείαν ἑκάστου φαρμάκου δύναμιν ἀφορίζων τοῖς κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς, ἔν τε τῷ τρίτῳ περὶ κράσεων βιβλίῳ καθόλου διεληλύθαμεν, ἔν τε τῇ νῦν ἐνεστώσῃ πραγματείᾳ διὰ τῶν κατὰ μέρος ἐπε ξίωμεν, ὡς ἤδη κᾀν τῷ πρώτῳ βιβλίῳ πεποιήκαμεν ἐφ’ ὕδατος καὶ ὄξους· ἐνταυθοῖ δὲ πάλιν, ἐπ’ ἐλαίου τε καὶ ῥοδίνου πράξομεν, ἐνδειξάμενοι πρότερον ἡλίκον ἁμαρτάνουσιν οἱ πόῤῥωθεν τῶν ἰατρῶν ἀποδείξεων ἀρχόμενοι. τί γὰρ ἔδει τὴν χρόαν ἐλαίου σκοπεῖν, ἢ ῥόδων, ἔχοντάς γε αὐτοῖς χρῆσθαι τοῖς πράγμασιν πολυειδῶς, οὐκ ἐπὶ τῶν ὑγιαινόντων μόνον; ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν νοσούντων σωμάτων;
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοῖς κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς, ἐπεξίωμεν
But since some things do escape them, one must try to bring reasoning to bear upon these, yet neither from afar, as those men do, nor syllogising inconclusively, but first 463 dividing precisely the significations of the names, so that no deception arises from homonymy in what follows; and second, distinguishing what acts in itself from what acts incidentally. Both of these have been discussed precisely in what precedes, and the significations of things said to be hot in potentiality, or cold, or dry, or moist, or anything else whatever, have been divided. And how one may discover the proper power of each drug, marking it off from those that are incidental (τοῖς κατὰ συμβεβηκός), we have gone through in general in the third book of On Mixtures, and in the treatise now in hand we go through it by means of the particulars, as we have already done in the first book in the case of water and vinegar; and here again we shall do so in the case of olive oil and rose oil, having first shown how great an error is committed by those physicians who begin their demonstrations from afar. For what need was there to examine the colour of oil, or of roses, when one is able to use the things themselves in many ways, not only on healthy bodies but also on diseased ones?
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπε ξίωμεν
But since some things do escape them, one must try to bring reason to bear on them, though neither from afar, as those people do, nor by drawing inconclusive syllogisms, but first 463 by precisely distinguishing the meanings of the names, so that no deception arises in what follows through homonymy; and second, by separating off that which acts in some way per se from that which acts per accidens. Both of these have been discussed precisely in what precedes, and the meanings of things said to be potentially hot, or cold, or dry, or moist, or to be anything else whatever, have been distinguished. And how one might discover the proper power of each drug, separating it off from what is per accidens, we have gone through in general terms in the third book On Mixtures, and in the present treatise we go through it by way of particulars, as we already did in the first book in the case of water and vinegar; and here again we shall do so in the case of oil and rose oil, having first shown how greatly those physicians err who begin their demonstrations from afar. For why should one examine the colour of oil, or of roses, when one is able to use the things themselves in many ways, not only on healthy bodies but also on diseased ones?

465 In this respect, then, they seem to me to err in no small way, and still more than this in those matters where they babble inconclusive and undemonstrated things, being ignorant of demonstrative method. For let fire be assumed to be either yellow or red, or however they wish, for it makes no difference to me. What, then, has this to do with everything red being hot? For it must be said, if it so happens, that all fire is red; but to assert that everything red is fire is ridiculous, and still more so if someone were to say that everything red is hot. If it had been assumed from the outset in the argument that everything hot is red, it would perhaps have been possible, by twisting the proposition, to declare everything red to be hot—though erring manifestly in this too; for it is the propositions that convert, not those that are inverted, that are true together with one another; but nonetheless the fallacy would, I think, have been close at hand. But since not even this itself has been assumed at the start—I mean that everything hot is red—it is not possible, even by inverting it, to say that everything red is hot.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀντιστρέφειν αὐτῷ, ἀντιστρέφοντας, ἀναστρέφοντας
In three ways, then, those who assert that roses are hot in their mixture, 466 because they are also red, go wrong in their argument. First, because, without having shown that all fire is red, they attempt to convert with it another statement, that everything red is fire, as if it were true; second, because they do not know that it is the propositions that convert with (ἀντιστρέφοντας) true propositions, not those that reverse them (ἀναστρέφοντας), that are themselves also true; and third, because even if they do convert, what will be concluded from the conversion is not that everything red in colour is hot, but a different proposition of the same kind, that everything red is fire. And that such a proposition is not only useless for the present purpose but also manifestly false is clear to everyone. For what we need to have demonstrated is not whether everything red is fire, but that it is hot. Since if indeed everything red is fire, then clearly the rose too will be fire for us. But physicians who, I think, do not know the logical methods go most seriously astray first of all in this very point, that they overstep their proper boundaries.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀντιστρέφειν αὐτῷ ... πειρῶνται λόγον ἕτερον, ὡς ἀληθῆ (construction is awkward and possibly corrupt), ἀντιστρέφοντας / ἀναστρέφοντας (the exact logical distinction intended: conversion versus inversion), ἀντιστρέφωσιν (in the third point Galen appears to use 'convert' loosely for the inversion he has just criticised)
Those who assert that roses are hot in their mixture, 466 because they are also red, therefore err in three ways in their argument. First, because, without having shown that all fire is red, they try to make another proposition convert with it, namely that everything red is fire, as though it were true. Second, because they do not know that it is the propositions that convert, not those that are inverted, which are themselves true along with true propositions. And third, because even if they do convert, what will be concluded from the conversion is not that everything red in colour is hot, but some other proposition of this kind, different from it: that everything red is fire. That such a proposition is not only useless for the present purposes but also manifestly false is clear to everyone. For what we need to have demonstrated is not whether everything red is fire, but that it is hot. Indeed, if everything red is fire, then evidently the rose too will be fire for us. But physicians who do not know the logical methods, I think, go most gravely wrong first of all in this very thing: in overstepping their own proper boundaries.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὸ πρὸς τοῦτο ἐξεταζόμενον
For experience, together with brief epilogisms, is sufficient to discover the powers of drugs; and then, in their arguments too, they err in ways similar to children who have just learned 467 logical theory. Those too are caught in the same fault who infer something about the power of drugs from their odours. For neither is everything fragrant hot, as some say, not knowing how one must understand what has been said by Aristotle and Theophrastus on these matters—which we shall define in the fourth of these commentaries—nor is everything hot fragrant; nor indeed is everything foul-smelling hot, nor, if something is hot, is it foul-smelling. So too, if someone should say that everything foul-smelling is cold, or that what is cold is foul-smelling, he does not speak the truth. For indeed among hot things countless ones are fragrant, others foul-smelling, and others neither; and likewise among cold things. And yet why do I say "cold" and "hot", when we ought to say "heating" and "cooling", which are in fact what we are investigating? For it is certainly not what is hot without qualification, or what is examined in relation to this, that it is proposed to investigate now, but what heats the human body. And likewise cold not without qualification, but whatever cools a human being.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πρὸς τούτων
So here again, not having distinguished the significations of the names, 468 they go wrong because of the homonymy, just as if someone, while discoursing about the land dog, should pass over in his argument to the sea dog, thinking that, just as the name of both animals is one, so also their nature is one. But it has been shown clearly by us in the commentaries On Mixture, which we said right at the beginning should precede this treatise, that what is hot in potentiality is one thing, and what is hot in actuality another; and that, of this hot in actuality, one kind is said to be hot simply, another by predominance, another in relation to the well-proportioned thing of the same kind, and another in relation to anything whatever that happens to be at hand. And indeed we said that man is hot not simply, as fire is, but hot by predominance; for there is in him a greater portion of the hot than of the cold. Accordingly, all the things that are said by these people to be hot in potentiality will be so called in virtue of their heating him. And the judgment of them is both very easy and will be tested through experience, requiring, as was also said before, brief epilogisms.

The sophists, then, passing over these matters, both waste the time of the young 469 and, leading astray by sophisms men unexercised in the solution of sophisms, fill them with false doctrines. For they ought to have made mention neither of colour nor of odour, nor indeed of consistency, or smoothness, or roughness, or anything else of the kind. For what does it matter that oil is viscous, or that it is smooth, or that it is glistening, or wet, or pale-yellow, or oily, or slippery, or hard to dry when smeared upon bodies? For from none of such things is it possible to infer whether it is hot, or cold, or dry, or moist in its power. For indeed, among viscous things and smooth things and glistening things, some manifestly heat us, others cool, and some dry, others moisten. And in respect of each of the accidents belonging not to oil alone but to all the other things as well, there is nothing to be found fixed or constant, such that one could establish upon it some universal premise.

For I pass over saying that in all such arguments the sophists do not even employ scientific [methods] 470 for the discovery of the premises, but [argue] from induction; and, what is worst of all, that they have never even inquired into the nature of induction, nor, should anyone challenge them to this, do they dare to follow, but they even censure those who occupy themselves at all with demonstration. For I pass over the fact that in all such matters the sophists do not even use scientific arguments 470 for finding the lemmas, but argue from induction. Worst of all, they have never once examined the nature of induction, and they do not dare to follow even if someone challenges them to it; they even censure those who devote some labour to demonstration. Likewise, the man who says it is hot because it easily bursts into flame, like bitumen, offers a demonstration of equal force with that of the man who says it is cold because it easily congeals, like water.

Greek (Kühn)
τὸ γὰρ ἐκ παραδείγματος ἢ ἐκ παραδειγμάτων πιστοῦσθαί τι διαφέρει τοῦ δι’ ἐπαγωγῆς πιστουμένου τῷ τὸν μὲν ἐκ παραδειγμάτων πιστούμενον ἓν μὲν ἢ δύο τῶν ὁμογενῶν λέγειν, ἢ πάντως γε ὀλίγα τὰ πλείω παραλείποντα, τὸν δ’ ἐξ ἐπαγωγῆς ἅπαντα περιλαμβάνειν πειρᾶσθαι τὰ διὰ τῆς ἐμπειρίας ἐγνωσμένα, καὶ 471 μηδὲν ὡς οἷόν τε παραλιπεῖν ὃ προφανές τε καὶ δῆλον τοῖς πολλοῖς, ἀλλὰ ἀποκεκρυμμένον τε καὶ ὀλίγοις γνωστόν. ὅθεν καὶ βίαιός ἐστιν καὶ πείθει σφοδρῶς ἡ διὰ πάντων τῶν ἐξ ἐμπειρίας γινωσκομένων ἐπαγωγὴ καὶ μόνοις αὐτοῖς ἡ ἀλαζονεία κατάφωρος γίνεται τοῖς γεγυμνασμένοις ἐν ἀποδεικτικαῖς μεθόδοις. καὶ διὰ τοῦθ’ ἡμεῖς ἐπιπλέον ὑπὲρ αὐτῆς ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῆς ἀποδείξεως ὑπομνήμασιν διήλθομεν. ἴσως δ’ ἄν ποτε καὶ κατὰ μόνας ἓν ὑπὲρ αὐτῆς ἰδίᾳ συνθείημεν γράμμα. Φανεῖται δὲ καὶ νῦν οὐδὲν ἧττον ἡ φύσις αὐτῆς τε τῆς ἐπαγωγῆς καὶ πολὺ δὴ μᾶλλον ἡ τοῦ παραδείγματος. ἅπαντα γὰρ ἐπελθεῖν ἔγνωκα διὰ κεφαλαίων ὅσα τοῖς ἰατροῖς εἴρηται περὶ δυνάμεως ἐλαίου, πιθανῶς μὲν τῷ δοκεῖν, οὐ μὴν ἀληθῶς γε. καὶ πρῶτον τὸ ὑπὸ Διοκλέους ἐν Ἀρχιδάμῳ λελεγμένον, ὡς σκληρύνεσθαι καὶ ἐπικαίεσθαι τὸ δέρμα τοῖς ἐν ἐλαίῳ τριβομένοις, ὑπελάμβανεν ὁ Ἀρχίδαμος καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τὴν ξηροτριβίαν προὔκρινε.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μηδὲν ὡς οἷόν τε παραλιπεῖν ὃ προφανές τε καὶ δῆλον τοῖς πολλοῖς, ἀλλὰ ἀποκεκρυμμένον τε καὶ ὀλίγοις γνωστόν
For establishing something from an example or from examples differs from what is established through induction in this respect: the one who establishes from examples mentions one or two of the things of the same kind, or at any rate few, omitting the majority, whereas the one who proceeds from induction attempts to encompass everything that is known through experience, and 471 to omit, so far as possible, nothing that is obvious and clear to the many, but also what is hidden and known to few. Hence the induction that proceeds through all the things known from experience is forcible and persuades strongly, and the imposture becomes detectable only to those who have been trained in demonstrative methods. And for this reason we have gone through it at greater length in the commentaries On Demonstration; and perhaps one day we may also compose a single separate book on it by itself. But even now the nature of induction itself will appear no less, and that of the example much more so. For I have resolved to go through, under heads, everything that has been said by physicians about the power of oil, plausibly in appearance, but not truly. And first, what has been said by Diocles in the Archidamus, that Archidamus supposed the skin to be hardened and scorched in those who are rubbed with oil, and for this reason preferred dry rubbing.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μηδὲν ὡς οἷόν τε παραλιπεῖν ὃ προφανές τε καὶ δῆλον τοῖς πολλοῖς, ἀλλὰ ἀποκεκρυμμένον (construction elliptical; 'not only… but also' supplied), ἀλαζονεία, κατάφωρος, ἐπικαίεσθαι
For establishing something from an example, or from examples, differs from establishing it through induction in this: the one who establishes from examples cites one or two of the things of the same kind, or in any case a few, leaving out the greater number, whereas the one who argues from induction tries to take in all the things known through experience, and 471 to leave out, so far as possible, nothing: not [only] what is evident and plain to the many, but [also] what is hidden and known to few. Hence induction through all the things known from experience is both compelling and strongly persuasive, and its imposture becomes detectable only to those trained in demonstrative methods. For this reason we went through it at greater length in the commentaries On Demonstration; and perhaps at some time we may also compose a single work about it separately, on its own. Yet even now the nature of induction itself will be no less apparent, and much more so that of the example. For I have resolved to go through, under main headings, everything that has been said by the physicians about the power of oil, plausibly in appearance, but certainly not truly. And first, what was stated by Diocles in the Archidamus: that Archidamus supposed that the skin becomes hardened and scorched in those who are rubbed in oil, and for this reason preferred dry rubbing.

Greek (Kühn)
συνεκπυροῦσθαί τε γὰρ, φησὶ, καὶ ἐπικαίειν τὸ ἔλαιον ὑπὸ τῆς τρίψεως θερμαινόμενον 472 ἐνόμιζεν καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο ἐξικμάζειν καὶ ξηραίνειν ἱκανῶς, ὥσπερ τῶν ὀπτωμένων τὰ χριόμενα. καὶ γὰρ καὶ ταῦτα σκληρύνεσθαι μᾶλλον ἢ εἰ χωρὶς ἐλαίου τις ὀπτήσειεν. αὐτὴν δὲ ἴσως ἄμεινον ὅλην παραγράψαι τοῦ Διοκλέους τὴν ῥῆσιν. ἔχει δὲ ᾧδε· τὸ δὲ μετ’ ἐλαίου τρίβειν οὐχ ὁμοίως ἐδοκίμαζε. πρῶτον μὲν γὰρ ἀνώμαλον ᾤετο γίνεσθαι τὴν τοιαύτην τρίψιν παρὰ τὸ τὰς χεῖρας ὀλισθαίνειν καὶ μὴ δύνασθαι τῆς σαρκὸς ὁμοίως ἀντιλαμβάνεσθαι διὰ τὸ λίπος. ἔτι δὲ καὶ σκληρύνεσθαι καὶ ἐπικαίεσθαι τὸ δέρμα τοῖς οὕτω τριβομένοις ἔφη μᾶλλον ἢ τοῖς ξηροῖς. συνεκπυροῦσθαι γὰρ καὶ ἐπικαίειν τὸ ἔλαιον θερμαινόμενον ὑπὸ τῆς τρίψεως, καθάπερ τῶν ὀπτωμένων τὰ χριόμενα τῶν μὴ χριομένων, ἐκπυρουμένου τοῦ ἐλαίου θερμαινόμενα καὶ ἐξικμαζόμενα λίαν σκληρύνεσθαι μᾶλλον. ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ τὰ ἐν τῷ ἐλαίῳ ἑψόμενα κραῦρα καὶ καπυρὰ γίνεσθαι διὰ τὴν αὐτὴν αἰτίαν. πρὸς δὲ τούτοις, ὥσπερ τὰ ξύλα καὶ τὰ δέρματα καὶ τὰ ἄλλα τὰ μετ’ ἐλαίου τριβόμενα συνδιαδίδωσιν εἴσω τὸ ἔλαιον, οὕτως ᾤετο καὶ τὰ σώματα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συνεκπυροῦσθαι, καπυρὰ
For he thought, he says, that the oil, being heated by the rubbing, 472 is set ablaze together with the skin and burns it, and that in this way it draws out the moisture and dries it considerably, just as, among things that are roasted, those that have been anointed. For these too become harder than if one roasted them without oil. But perhaps it is better to copy out Diocles' statement itself in full. It runs as follows: "Rubbing with oil he did not approve of in the same way. For first he thought that such rubbing becomes uneven, because the hands slip and cannot take hold of the flesh in the same way on account of the grease. Further, he said that the skin is hardened and burnt more in those rubbed in this manner than in those rubbed dry. For the oil, being heated by the rubbing, is set ablaze together with it and burns, just as, among things that are roasted, those that have been anointed more than those not anointed, since, as the oil is set ablaze, they are heated and have their moisture drawn out excessively and are hardened the more. In the same way too things boiled in oil become brittle and crisp for the same cause. In addition to this, just as wood and hides and the other things that are rubbed with oil let the oil pass through into the interior, so he thought bodies do as well."
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συνεκπυροῦσθαι, ἐκπυρουμένου, κραῦρα, καπυρὰ, συνδιαδίδωσιν
For he thought, he says, that the oil, being heated by the rubbing, becomes fiery along with [the body] and scorches, 472 and in this way draws out the moisture and dries considerably, just as, among things being roasted, those that are anointed do. For these too become harder than if one were to roast them without oil. But perhaps it is better to copy out the whole statement of Diocles itself. It runs thus: 'Rubbing with oil he did not approve of in the same way. For in the first place he thought that such rubbing turns out uneven, because the hands slip and cannot take hold of the flesh evenly on account of the fat. Further, he said that the skin is also hardened and scorched in those rubbed in this way more than in those rubbed dry. For the oil, heated by the rubbing, becomes fiery along with it and scorches, just as, among things being roasted, those that are anointed, being heated as the oil becomes fiery and having their moisture drawn out excessively, become harder than those not anointed. Likewise things boiled in oil also become brittle and crisp for the same cause. In addition to these, just as wood and hides and the other things rubbed with oil let the oil pass through inwards along with them, so, he thought, do bodies also.'

And when this happens, 473 many of the things that are accustomed to flow through the flesh together with the pneuma and to pass out are held back, just as also through strainers and linen cloths and wools and all the things through which something is strained, when oil is poured on and smeared over them, the liquids cannot flow in the same way. And when such excretion is intercepted, some of them flow back inward, and, gathering together, fill up the pores, being entangled with one another and enclosed by the oil because the oil has something glutinous in it, just as in other cases the oily substance is seen to catch up dust and chaff and other such things. And as the pores are continually and little by little obstructed, it is necessary that many of the things accustomed to come about according to nature are accomplished worse. And when this happens, 473 many of the things that are accustomed to flow through the flesh together with the pneuma and to pass out are, he thought, shut in; just as through strainers and linen cloths and wool, and through all the things through which something poured on or smeared on is strained, liquids cannot flow in the same way. And when such excretion is intercepted, some of it flows back inward, and, collecting, fills up the pores, being interwoven with one another and enveloped by the oil, because the oil has something viscid about it, just as in other cases the oily is seen to catch up dust, bits of chaff and other such things. And as the pores are continually and little by little obstructed, it is necessary that many of the things accustomed to take place according to nature be accomplished worse. He also supposed that it has something biting in it, on account of which it makes the eyes shed tears, makes the throat rough, scrapes the belly and, when drunk, produces bloody evacuations.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Those, then, who anoint themselves without rubbing it does not trouble at all; for the biting is too weak to produce any sensation; but with rubbing, 474 as it penetrates into the flesh, it does more harm by its biting, just as many drugs, when merely smeared on in this way, are less strong, but when pressed in and penetrating inward act more powerfully. And indeed with nettles and many other things, those who touch them gently are not troubled at all, but those who strike against them and press on them are pained. These, then, are the things that Diocles says in the Archidamus. And it is possible to consider at once both what is plausible in them and what is faulty. For that anything whatever that is boiled in oil loses its own proper moisture and quickly becomes friable and brittle, while it suffers nothing of the kind in water, we have been taught by experience, so that in this respect Archidamus stated nothing false. But whereas he ought, just as Aristotle and Theophrastus and certain other philosophers propound and solve such problems in their physical inquiries, himself also, if indeed he wished to seek the causes of everything that comes about, to propound and solve them in the same way as they do; since he does not appear to be doing this, it is in this above all that he seems to me to go wrong.

This, then, is what Diocles says in the Archidamus. One may examine at the same time what is plausible in these statements and what is faulty. But Aristotle and Theophrastus, and certain other philosophical men, propose and solve problems of this kind in their inquiries into nature. He too, if he really wished to seek the causes of all things that come about, ought to have proposed and solved them in the same way as they do. And if he is evidently not doing this, it is in this above all, it seems to me, that he goes wrong.

For what appears evidently must always 475 be posited first, and next, if one wishes, its cause must be examined, composing natural problems in some such manner as this. Why does olive oil make the things boiled down in it dry and crumbly and brittle, whereas water makes them moist and soft? Or why do things boiled in oil dry out, whereas things otherwise steeped in it preserve their own proper moisture more than if they had not been steeped at all? And why does it cure the bitings from squill and nettle? And why is it curative of these, while it itself bites the eyes? Why does it not bite all the rest of the body, but bites the eyes? Why does it roughen the pharynx, but soothe ulcers? Why is it laxative of the belly? Why is it curative of fatigues? Why does it keep those who anoint themselves from being chilled? Why is it destructive of insect animals? Why is it softening of straps and hides, but hardening of almost all things that are boiled in it? This is one kind of problems for you, belonging to the natural philosopher alone. The other, which is now common also to those from the arts, is as follows.

For what appears evidently must always 475 be laid down first, and only afterwards, if one so wishes, should its cause be examined, by composing physical problems in the following way. Why does oil make the things boiled down in it dry, friable and brittle, whereas water makes them moist and soft? Or why are things boiled in oil dried out, while things otherwise steeped in it keep their proper moisture better than if they had not been steeped at all? And why does it heal the bitings from squill and nettle? And why is it curative of these, yet itself bites the eyes? Why does it not bite all the rest of the body, but bites the eyes? Why does it roughen the pharynx, yet soothe ulcers? Why does it draw down the belly? Why is it remedial of fatigues? Why does it keep those who are to be anointed free from chilling? Why is it destructive of insects? Why is it softening of thongs and hides, but hardening of almost all the things boiled in it? This is for you one kind of problems, befitting the natural philosopher alone. The other, which is now common also to those who come from the arts, is of this sort.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For what reason do cooks, when roasting fish and meat, first smear them 476 with oil? Why do the gymnastic trainers rub athletes with oil? And why do the physical instructors also rub the boys? For it is possible to give an empirical cause for each of such problems: the cooks would say that they smear the fish and the meat for this reason, that their outer parts become dry and scorched round about when they are roasted without fat; the gymnastic trainers would say that they rub the athletes in oil because this both dissolves the fatigues already present and mitigates those to come, and makes the rubbing itself perfectly even and gentle, and prepares the body for the movements to come. And for these same reasons the physical instructors too will say that they anoint the boys both before the exercises and after the exercises. Why, however, oil is a remedy for fatigues, neither a gymnastic trainer nor a physical instructor nor an Empiricist physician knows any longer. Yet it is not the case that, because they do not know, they abandon what evidently appears.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐκ ἔτι
For what reason do cooks, when they roast fish and meat, first anoint 476 them with oil? Why do trainers rub athletes with oil? And why do the masters of the palaestra (paidotribai) do so to boys as well? For it is possible to give an empirical cause for each of such problems: the cooks would say that they anoint the fish and the meat for this reason, that their outer parts become dry and scorched all round when they are roasted without the fat; the trainers would say that they rub the athletes in oil because it both dissolves the fatigues already present and soothes those that are to come, and renders the rubbing itself even and perfectly gentle, and prepares the body for the movements that are to follow. And for these same reasons the masters of the palaestra too will say that they anoint the boys both before the exercises and after the exercises. Why, however, oil is a remedy for fatigues, this neither trainer nor master of the palaestra nor Empiricist physician knows any further. Yet neither do they, because they do not know it, abandon what is evidently apparent.

For not even knowing why white hellebore purges upward and black downward, 477 nor knowing why cnecus is evacuative of phlegm and epithymum of black humours, they nevertheless use the drugs for those purposes which experience has taught, and they treat those in need, and they trust what is evidently apparent, and they laugh at those who establish the contrary by reasoning. But Archidamus, in addition to his disdaining, in many matters, what is evidently apparent, seems to me to deserve justly to be censured in this respect as well. For he says that dry rubbing is better than that with oil, because it renders the body harder and drier in those who are anointed, whereas it becomes softer in those who are rubbed dry. Yet no less, in this same discourse, after proceeding a little further, he says that wood and hides and the other things that are rubbed with oil let it pass through as far as their depth, and that this is the greatest testimony that in our bodies too, passing inward, it plasters up the pores and prevents the outflows of the moist humours. And I for my part do not know how these things agree.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὸ ἀποκρινόμενον, χέον
For if oil prevents the outflows of the fluids, it would surely not be said to dry, so that 478 it is in vain that it is guarded against, as something drying, in the rubbings. And he seems to me to err also in thinking that those who are rubbed in oil are disposed in a similar way to things boiled in it. For he surely does not say that hot, or cold, or well-tempered, or lukewarm, or boiling oil does the same things, just as water itself does not either. For this too evidently, if it is applied cold, condenses and compacts and contracts and constricts what is being excreted, just as, if it is boiling to the extreme, it burns; but if it is applied well-tempered and moderately hot, it relaxes and rarefies and liquefies and disperses. But if it is neither markedly cold nor hot, but such as what is called by most people spring-water, then it does none of the things mentioned, but displays its own proper power. So too, then, if anyone wishes to examine oil, let him first render it free of all acquired coldness and heat, and in this way make the test.

For if oil prevents the outflows of the fluids, it could surely not be said to dry; so that 478 it is in vain that it is avoided in rubbings as something drying. So, then, if anyone wishes to examine oil as well, let him first make it free of all acquired cooling and heat, and in that state carry out the test.

K11.478-479K. XI 478–479, ch. 2.6 difficulty mid (2.27/100; thin 4, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 5/7 (c) pass 13/14 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
τὸ μὲν γὰρ ἄκρως ψυχρὸν οὐ κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ μόνον ἔτι δύναμιν, ἀλλὰ καὶ διά τὴν τῆς ὀθνείας ποιότητος ἐπικράτησιν ἐνεργήσει τι περὶ τοῖς πλησιάζουσιν: ﻿ 479 οὕτως δὲ καὶ τὸ θερμὸν ἱκανῶς. εἰ δ’ ἀμφοτέρων ἔξω καταστήσας αὐτὸ βασανίζειν ἐθέλοις, ἐξεύροις ἂν οὕτως μόνως τὴν οἰκείαν ἐλαίου δύναμιν. ὥσθ’ ὅτε μὲν ἐλαίῳ συνήθως τρίβεταί τις, ὥσπερ οἱ γυμναστικοὶ πάντες, ἐν ἐλαίῳ μόνῳ τρίβεται, πάσης ἄνωθεν τῆς ποιότητος ἔξωθεν. ὅταν δ’ ἤτοι ζέον ἢ θερμὸν ἱκανῶς ἐργασάμενος, ἢ καὶ ψυχρὸν ἐσχάτως αὐτὸ, χρῷτο τηνικαῦτα, σύνθετον ἀνάγκη γίνεσθαι τὴν ἐνέργειαν ἔκ τε τῆς οἰκείας ἐλαίου δυνάμεως καὶ τῆς ἐπικτήτου ποιότητος. καὶ πολλάκις δὲ κατακρύπτεσθαι τελέως ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν τὴν οἰκείαν δύναμιν ἑκάστῳ τῶν φαρμάκων, ὅταν ἰσχυρὰν πάνυ προσλάβῃ τὴν ἔξωθεν ποιότητα. τί τοίνυν ὅμοιον ἢ ἑψεῖν ἐν ἐλαίῳ ζέοντι βοτάνας, ἢ τὰ τῶν γυμναζομένων ἀνατρίβειν σώματα; μάλιστα μὲν γὰρ, ὡς καὶ πρόσθεν ἐπιδέδεικται, φεύγειν χρὴ τὰς ἀπὸ τῶν ἔξωθεν πίστεις, ἔχοντάς γε τὴν οἰκείαν ἑκάστῳ προσφέρειν πεῖραν. εἰ δ’ ἄρα ποτὲ καὶ τοῦτο πράττειν ἐθελήσειέ τις, ἀλλ’ ὅμοιά γε παραβάλλειν πειρᾶσθαι, καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῶν βυρσῶν ἐποίησεν καὶ τῶν ἱμάντων.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πάσης ἄνωθεν τῆς ποιότητος ἔξωθεν, ἄνωθεν
For that which is extremely cold will no longer act upon the things that come near it according to its own power alone, but also through the predominance of the foreign quality; 479 and so too that which is sufficiently hot. But if you are willing to set it outside both of these and then test it, only thus would you discover the proper power of oil. So that when someone is rubbed with oil in the customary way, as all those devoted to gymnastics are, he is rubbed in oil alone, with every quality from without kept away from the outset. But when someone, having made it either boiling or sufficiently hot, or even extremely cold, then uses it, the action must necessarily become composite, from both the proper power of oil and the acquired quality. And often it is necessary that the proper power of each of the drugs be completely hidden, when it has taken on a very strong quality from without. What similarity, then, is there between boiling herbs in boiling oil and rubbing down the bodies of those who exercise? For above all, as has also been shown before, one must avoid proofs drawn from external things, when one is in a position to apply to each thing its own proper test. But if ever someone should wish to do even this, let him at least try to compare similar things, as he did in the case of the hides and the straps.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πάσης ἄνωθεν τῆς ποιότητος ἔξωθεν, ἄνωθεν, γυμναστικοὶ
For what is extremely cold will act upon the things that come near it no longer according to its own power alone, but also through the predominance of the foreign quality; 479 and so too what is sufficiently hot. But if you wish to test it after placing it outside both of these, only in this way would you discover the proper power of oil. So, when someone is rubbed with oil in the customary way, as all who practise gymnastics are, he is rubbed with oil alone, every quality from outside being absent from the start. But when someone uses it after having made it either boiling or sufficiently hot, or else extremely cold, then the action must necessarily be compound, arising both from the proper power of oil and from the acquired quality. And often, indeed, the proper power of each of the drugs must be completely concealed, when it takes on a very strong quality from outside. What likeness, then, is there between boiling herbs in boiling oil and rubbing down the bodies of those who exercise? For above all, as has also been shown before, one must avoid proofs drawn from external things, at least when one is able to apply to each thing the test proper to it. But if someone should after all wish to do this too, let him at least try to compare like with like, as he did in the case of the hides and the straps.

οὔτε γὰρ προθερμήνας ἔλαιον οὔτ’ 480 ἐν χιόνι προψύξας οὐδεὶς, ἀλλὰ τὸ ἐπιτυχὸν ἕκαστος λαμβάνων ἱμάντας ἀνατρίβει καὶ βύρσας, ὥσπερ, οἶμαι, καὶ τοὺς παῖδας οἱ παιδοτρίβαι, κατὰ τὸ ἐπιτυχὸν ἔλαιον ἀνατρίβουσιν, οὔτε προθερμήναντες ἐπὶ πυρὸς οὔτε προψύξαντες ἐπὶ χιόνος. ἐπεί τοι καὶ ἡμεῖς οὐκ ὀλιγάκις ἐγκαιομένην κεφαλὴν ἐλαίῳ πάνυ ψυχρῷ καταντλήσαντες ἐνεψύξαμεν. εἰ δὲ μηδ’ ἁλῶν ἔχοι τοὔλαιον, ἔτι καὶ μᾶλλον ἐμψύξει. εἰ δὲ καὶ ὠμοτριβὲς εἴη, πολὺ δὴ καὶ μᾶλλον ἔτι. Σκευάζομεν δὲ καὶ τοῦτο παραπλησίως τῷ ὕδατι θερμήναντες γὰρ αὐτὸ πρότερον, οὕτως ἐνιστῶμεν ἀκραιφνεῖ ψυχρῷ. εἰ δὲ καὶ κρουνὸς ὑπερκείμενος ἐπ’ αὐτῷ καταράττων εἴη, προεστεγνωμένου δηλονότι τοῦ περιέχοντος ἀγγείου τοὔλαιον, ἔτι καὶ μᾶλλον ἂν οὕτω ψυχρότερον ἐργασθείη. ψυχρὸν δ’ ἱκανῶς γίνεται, κᾀπειδὰν προθερμανθὲν ἐν φρέατι κρεμασθὲν ψαῦον τοῦ ὕδατος. γίγνεται μὲν γὰρ καὶ τὸ μὴ ψαῦον ψυχρὸν, ἀλλ’ ἧττον. αἱ μὲν δὴ τοιαῦται παρασκευαὶ ψυχρὸν ἀποδεικνύουσι τοὔλαιον, αἱ δ’ ἐναντίαι θερμὸν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὠμοτριβὲς, προεστεγνωμένου
For no one, having first warmed the oil or 480 first cooled it in snow, but each taking whatever oil comes to hand, rubs down straps and hides, just as, I think, the physical instructors also rub down the boys with whatever oil comes to hand, neither having first warmed it on a fire nor first cooled it on snow. Since indeed we ourselves, not infrequently, have cooled a burning head by pouring very cold oil over it. And if the oil should contain no salt, it will cool still more. And if it should also be pressed from unripe olives (ὠμοτριβές), much more so still. We prepare this too in much the same way as water: for having first warmed it, we then set it in unmixed cold water. And if there should also be a spout set above it, dashing down upon it—the vessel containing the oil having obviously been stopped tight beforehand—it would in this way be made colder still. And it becomes sufficiently cold also when, having first been warmed, it is hung in a well touching the water. For even that which does not touch the water becomes cold, but less so. Such preparations, then, render the oil cold, and the opposite ones render it hot.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὠμοτριβὲς, προεστεγνωμένου, κρουνὸς ὑπερκείμενος ἐπ’ αὐτῷ καταράττων
For no one warms the oil beforehand or 480 chills it beforehand in snow; each takes whatever comes to hand and rubs down straps and hides with it. In the same way, I think, the masters of the palaestra (paidotribai) rub down the boys with whatever oil comes to hand, neither warming it beforehand over a fire nor chilling it beforehand on snow. Indeed, we ourselves have not infrequently cooled a burning head by pouring very cold oil over it. And if the oil has no salt in it either, it will cool still more; and if it is also pressed from unripe olives (ōmotribes), very much more still. We prepare this too in a similar way to water: having first heated it, we then set it in unmixed cold water. And if there is also a jet of water above it dashing down upon it, the vessel containing the oil having of course first been made watertight, it would in this way be made colder still. It also becomes sufficiently cold when, after first being heated, it is hung in a well touching the water. For it becomes cold even when it does not touch the water, but less so. Such preparations, then, render the oil cold, and the opposite ones render it hot.

χρώμεθα δὲ κᾀκείναις, ἐπὶ τῶν κατεψυγμένων σωμάτων, 481 εἰς δεξαμενὴν ἐμβιβάζοντες ἐλαίου θερμοῦ τοὺς ἀπονεναρκωμένους τι μέρος, ἢ τρομώδεις, ἢ σπασμώδεις, ἢ παλμώδεις, ἢ ἀκινήτους, ἢ ἀναισθήτους, ἢ δυσαισθήτους, ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερον πάσχοντας. εἴ τις τοίνυν ἐξ ἁπάσης χρήσεως ἀποφαίνοιτο περὶ τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως, οὐκ ἂν φθάνοι καὶ θερμαντικὸν ἡμῶν καὶ ψυκτικὸν οἰόμενος εἶναι τοὔλαιον, ὑγραντικόν τε καὶ ξηραντικὸν, ὥσπερ καὶ ὁ τοῦ Διοκλέους Ἀρχίδαμος, εἰ καὶ μὴ παρακολουθεῖ ταῖς ἐναντιολογίαις. ὅταν μὲν γὰρ ξηραίνεσθαι καὶ κραῦρα γίνεσθαι λέγῃ τὰ ἐν τῷ ἐλαίῳ ἑψόμενα, ξηραντικήν τινα καὶ διαφορητικὴν ἐπιδείκνυσιν αὐτοῦ τὴν δύναμιν· ὅταν δ’ εἴσω τῶν βυρσῶν δύεσθαι καὶ τοὺς ἡμετέρους ἐμπλάττειν πόρους καὶ κωλύειν τὰς φυσικὰς τῶν ὑγρῶν ἐκροὰς, ἐναντιωτάτην δήπου πάλιν ἐκ τούτων ἐπιδείκνυσι τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ τοῖς διαφοροῦσί τε καὶ ξηραίνουσιν. ἀλλ’ ἐν τούτοις μὲν ἔτι τὰ γοῦν φαινόμενα λέγων οὐκ ὀρθῶς ὑπὲρ αὐτῶν συλλογίζεται. κατὰ δὲ τὸν περὶ τῆς δήξεως αὐτοῦ λόγον οὐδὲ ταῦτα διέρχεται πάντα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὁ τοῦ Διοκλέους Ἀρχίδαμος, παρακολουθεῖ
And we use the latter too, in the case of bodies that have been chilled, 481 putting into a tank of warm oil those who have become numb in some part, or are tremulous, or spasmodic, or subject to palpitation, or immobile, or insensible, or of impaired sensation, or suffering some other such thing. If, then, someone were to make pronouncements about the power of drugs on the basis of every use, he would straightway come to think that oil is for us both heating and cooling, both moistening and drying, just as Diocles' Archidamus does too, even if he does not follow out his own contradictions. For when he says that things boiled in oil are dried out and become brittle, he shows its power to be a drying and dispersing one; but when he says that it sinks into hides and plasters up our pores and prevents the natural outflows of the moist humours, from these he surely shows in turn that its power is most contrary to those things that disperse and dry. But in these matters, while at least still stating the phenomena, he does not reason correctly about them; whereas in his account of its biting quality he does not even go through all of these phenomena.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὁ τοῦ Διοκλέους Ἀρχίδαμος, παρακολουθεῖ ταῖς ἐναντιολογίαις, οὐκ ἂν φθάνοι, ἀπονεναρκωμένους
We also use the latter, in the case of chilled bodies, 481 placing into a tank of hot oil those who have become numbed in some part, or are tremulous, or spasmodic, or palpitating, or immobile, or without sensation, or with impaired sensation, or suffering some other such thing. If, then, someone were to pronounce on the power of drugs on the basis of every use, he would straightaway think that oil is both heating and cooling for us, and both moistening and drying, just as Diocles' Archidamus does, even if he does not follow through the contradictions. For when he says that the things boiled in oil are dried and become brittle, he shows a certain drying and dispersing power in it; but when he says that it sinks into hides, plugs our pores and prevents the natural outflows of the fluids, from these he again shows its power to be, surely, most opposite to things that disperse and dry. In these matters, however, while he at least still states the phenomena, he does not reason correctly about them. But in his account of its biting he does not even go through all of them.

τὸ μὲν γὰρ τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς δάκνειν καὶ κερχνώδη 482 τὴν φάρυγγα ποιεῖν καὶ καταξύειν τὴν γαστέρα καὶ τὰς διαχωρήσεις αἱματώδεις ἐπιφέρειν, ἱκανὰ μαρτύρια τοῦ δακνώδη δύναμιν ὑπάρχειν αὐτῷ. τὸ δὲ καὶ τοῖς ἕλκεσιν ἀνώδυνον εἶναι καὶ τὰς τῆς γαστρὸς δήξεις, ἐπιβρεχόμενόν τε καὶ ἐνιέμενον πάντως ἰᾶσθαι καὶ τοὺς ἀπὸ τῶν ὀδαξησμῶν ἐρεθισμοὺς πραΰνειν, οὐ σμικρὰ πάλιν οὐδ’ αὐτὰ τῆς ἐναντίας αὐτοῦ δυνάμεως γνωρίσματα, παραλέλειπται τελέως. εἰ δ’ ἀμφοτέρων λεχθέντων ἐφεξῆς ἐθέλοι τις συλλογίσασθαι δυοῖν θάτερον, ἢ ὡς ἐμπειρικὸς ἐν τῷ πρός τι φήσει τὴν δύναμιν ἑκάστου τῶν φαρμάκων ἐξ ἐμπειρίας γινώσκειν, αὐτοῦ δὲ καθ’ ἑαυτὸ τὴν οἰκείαν φύσιν ἀγνοεῖν, ἢ ὡς λογικὸς τὰς ἐναντίας δυνάμεις ὑπάρχειν ταὐτῷ φαρμάκῳ, δακνώδη τε καὶ πραϋντικὴν, εἶτ’ οἶμαι μεγίστην ἀπορίαν ἕξει τὴν αἰτίαν ἀποδιδοὺς τοῦ ποτὲ μὲν τὴν ἑτέραν αὐτῶν ἐνεργεῖν αἰσθητῶς, ποτὲ δὲ τὴν ἑτέραν, ἢ ἀπολωλυίας, ἢ μὴ σαφῶς γε φαινομένης τῆς ἑτέρας.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For its biting the eyes and making the throat rough, 482 and scraping the belly and bringing on bloody evacuations, are sufficient testimonies that a biting power belongs to it. But that it is also painless for ulcers, and that it entirely cures the bitings of the belly when poured on and injected, and soothes the irritations arising from itchings—these too, in turn, being no small signs of its opposite power—has been completely left out. And if, once both have been stated in succession, someone should wish to draw a conclusion, one of two things follows: either, as an empiricist, he will say that he knows the power of each of the drugs from experience in relation to something, but is ignorant of its proper nature in itself; or, as a rationalist, that contrary powers, both biting and soothing, belong to the same drug—and then, I think, he will have the greatest difficulty in rendering the cause of why sometimes the one of them acts perceptibly and sometimes the other, the other being either destroyed or at any rate not clearly apparent.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κερχνώδη, ὀδαξησμῶν, ἐπιβρεχόμενόν, ἐν τῷ πρός τι
For its biting the eyes, making the throat rough 482, scraping the belly and bringing on bloody evacuations are sufficient testimonies that it possesses a biting power. But that it is also painless to ulcers, and that, poured on and injected, it in every case heals the bitings of the belly and soothes the irritations arising from itchings, which are themselves in turn no small signs of its opposite power, has been omitted altogether. And if, both having been stated one after the other, someone should wish to draw a conclusion, it will be one of two things: either, as an Empiric, he will say that he knows from experience the power of each of the drugs in relation to something, but is ignorant of its proper nature in and of itself; or, as a Rationalist, that opposite powers, biting and soothing, belong to the same drug; and then, I think, he will be in the greatest difficulty when he gives the cause why sometimes the one of them acts perceptibly and sometimes the other, the other having either perished or at any rate not being clearly apparent.

εἰσὶ δὲ τῶν τοιούτων ἁπασῶν ἀποριῶν αἱ λύσεις, εἰ μὲν ὡς φυσικὰ προβλήματα προβάλλοιντο, παγχάλεποί τινες, εἰ δ’ ὡς πρὸς τὴν χρείαν τῆς τέχνης ἀναφέροιντο, πείρας μὲν 483 πολλῆς, ἐπιλογισμῶν δὲ οὐ πολλῶν μὲν, ἀκριβῶν δὲ καὶ διωρισμένων δεόμεναι. πρῶτον μὲν γὰρ, εἰ πᾶν ἔλαιον τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς δακνῶδές ἐστιν ἢ μὴ τῇ πείρᾳ διορίσασθαι βέλτιον, οὐκ αὐτοσχεδιάζειν, ὥσπερ ῥήτορας ἐπιχειροῦντας εἰς ἑκάτερον. εἶθ’ ἑξῆς εἰ πᾶν ὁμοίως κερχνῶδες, ἢ γαστρὸς ὑπαγωγὸν ἢ καταξῦον αὐτὴν, ὡς αἱματώδεις ἐνίοτε κινεῖν ἐκκρίσεις, ἢ τὸ μέν τι ποιεῖ τῶν ἐλαίων ταῦτα, τὸ δ’ οὐ ποιεῖ, καὶ τὸ μὲν μᾶλλον, τὸ δ’ ἧττον. ἐλαίου δὲ διαφορὰς λέγω νῦν οὐχ ὡς ἄν τις ὑπονοήσῃ τὰς ἑτερογενεῖς, οἷον τοῦ κικίνου τε καὶ ῥαφανί νου καὶ καρυΐνου καὶ βαλανίνου καὶ τῶν ὁμοίων. εἰδέναι μὲν γὰρ χρὴ καὶ τὰς τούτων δυνάμεις τὸν ἰατρόν. ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἔν γε τῷ παρόντι προσήκει μνημονεύειν αὐτῶν. ἀρκεῖ γὰρ, οἶμαι, τήν γε πρώτην αὐτὸ τὸ κυριώτατον καὶ μάλιστα τὸ ὑπὸ πάντων ἀνθρώπων ὀνομαζόμενον ἔλαιον ἐπισκέψασθαι. καὶ γὰρ ὁ καρπὸς ἐξ οὗ γίγνεται τοῦτο, μόνος τῶν ἄλλων ἐλαία καὶ τὸ δένδρον ἐλαία προσαγορεύεται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
The solutions of all such puzzles, if they are posed as physical problems, are exceedingly difficult; but if they are referred to the use of the art, they require much experience 483, and epilogisms not many in number, but accurate and well-defined. For first, whether every oil is biting to the eyes or not, it is better to determine by experience, not to improvise, like orators arguing for either side. Then next, whether every oil is likewise throat-roughening, or laxative to the belly, or scrapes it so as sometimes to stir up bloody excretions, or whether one of the oils does these things and another does not, and one more and another less. By differences of oil I mean now not, as one might suppose, those of different kinds, such as castor oil and radish oil and walnut oil and balanos oil and the like. For the physician must indeed know the powers of these too; but it is not fitting at present to mention them. For it suffices, I think, in the first instance to examine that oil itself which is most properly and especially named 'oil' by all men. For indeed the fruit from which this is produced, alone among the rest, is called elaia (olive), and the tree too is called elaia.

The solutions of all such difficulties, if they were put forward as problems of natural philosophy, are extremely hard; but if they were referred to the use of the art, they require much experience 483, and inferences that are not many but precise and properly distinguished. For first, whether all oil is biting to the eyes or not is better determined by experience, not by improvising, like rhetors arguing on either side. Then next, whether all oil is likewise roughening, or a purgative of the belly, or scrapes it so as sometimes to set bloody excretions in motion; or whether one of the oils does these things and another does not, and one does so more and another less. By differences of oil I do not now mean, as one might suppose, those of a different kind, such as castor oil, radish oil, nut oil, balanos oil and the like. The physician must indeed know the powers of these too, but it is not fitting to mention them at present. For it suffices, I think, at the outset at least, to examine that which is oil in the most proper sense and is above all so named by all men. For the fruit from which this comes is, alone among the others, called olive, and the tree is also called olive.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And this 484 is called oil simply and in the primary sense, whereas all the others are so called by a kind of metaphor and catachresis. Of this oil itself, then, the one properly so named, about which we are conducting our entire discourse at present, I say that there are several differences: in respect of age, when it is either fresh and as it were must, or in its prime, or old; in respect of the manner of its preparation, when it is either bare and simple and on its own, or has been made with salt; in respect of the difference in the fruit itself, when the fruit is either ripe or unripe; and in respect of alteration brought about by artificial treatment, when it is either kept such as it happened to be prepared from the beginning, or has been whitened. Concerning its whitening not a few others have written, and it will also be discussed by us in what follows. Of all these oils I myself took pains to make trial of the power. And what I discovered in the case of each I shall relate after this; but now I wish first to bring to a conclusion the matters I set out upon.

And this 484 is called oil simply and in the primary sense, while all the others are so called by a kind of transference and catachresis. Greek (Kühn)
οὐκ ὀρθῶς 485 γὰρ δοκοῦσιν οἱ πλεῖστοι τῶν ἰατρῶν οὔτε πειραθῆναι φαρμάκων οὔτε συλλογίζεσθαι περὶ τῆς δυνάμεως αὐτῶν. Πειραθῆναι μὲν γὰρ χρὴ μόνων τῶν ἀμίκτων καὶ ἔξω πάσης ἐπικτήτου ποιότητος. ἐπὶ μὲν γὰρ τῆς ἀρίστης κατασκευῆς τοῦ σώματος πρῶτον, ἐφεξῆς δὲ κᾀπὶ τῶν δυσκράτων, εἶθ’ οὕτως ἐπὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν νοσημάτων, ὡς ἔν τε τῷ πρὸ τούτου λόγῳ κᾀν τῷ τρίτῳ περὶ κράσεως διῄρηται, συλλογίζεσθαι δὲ τὸ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ἀεὶ διορίζοντας τοῦ πρώτως τε καὶ καθ’ αὑτό. Σφάλλονται δ’ ἔνιοι τῶν ἰατρῶν οὐ περὶ ταῦτα μόνον, ἀλλὰ κᾀν ταῖς περὶ τῶν παθημάτων δόξαις. αὐτίκα τοὔλαιον, ὅτι μὲν ἴαμα κόπων ἐστὶν ἅπαντες σχεδὸν ὁμολογοῦσιν. ὁποῖον δέ τι πάθος ὁ κόπος ἐστὶν, οὐκέτι ὁμοδοξοῦσιν. ὁ μὲν γάρ τις ξηρότητα τῶν ἄρθρων εἶναί φησιν, ἐξικμασθέντων ἐν ταῖς κινήσεσιν, ὁ δέ τις καὶ τῶν μυῶν προστίθησιν, ἕτερος δέ τις ἔμπαλιν τούτοις ὑγρότητα πολλὴν καὶ τάσιν καὶ θερμασίαν, ἐπελθόντος γε ῥεύματος τοῖς σφοδρῶς κινηθεῖσι μέρεσιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For most physicians 485 seem neither to test drugs correctly nor to reason correctly about their power. For one must test only those that are unmixed and free of every acquired quality: first on the best constitution of the body, next also on ill-tempered bodies, and then in this way on simple diseases, as has been distinguished both in the book before this one and in the third book On Mixture; and one must reason, always distinguishing what is accidental from what is primary and per se. But some physicians go astray not only in these matters but also in their opinions about affections. Take olive oil, for example: that it is a remedy for fatigues nearly all agree; but what sort of affection fatigue is, they no longer agree. For one says it is a dryness of the joints, which have been drained of moisture in the movements; another adds also the muscles; and yet another, contrary to these, says it is much moisture and tension and warmth, a flux having come upon the parts that have been vigorously moved.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξικμασθέντων, κόπων, ῥεύματος
For most physicians do not, it seems, 485 go about correctly either testing drugs or reasoning about their power. One must test only those that are unmixed and free of every acquired quality: first on the best constitution of the body, next also on ill-tempered bodies, and then likewise on simple diseases, as has been set out both in the preceding book and in the third book On Mixtures. And one must reason while always distinguishing what is incidental from what is primary and per se. Some physicians go wrong not only in these matters but also in their opinions about the affections. Take oil, for instance: almost everyone agrees that it is a remedy for fatigues, but they no longer agree about what sort of affection fatigue is. One says that it is a dryness of the joints, which have been drained of their moisture in the movements; another adds the dryness of the muscles as well; and yet another, contrary to these, says it is a great moistness together with tension and warmth, because a flux has come upon the parts that were violently moved.

Greek (Kühn)
ἄλλος δὲ οὐ πλῆθος ὑγρῶν, ἀλλὰ 486 ποιότητα μόνην αἰτιᾶται, συντήξεις τινὰς φάμενος ἐν ταῖς ἀμετροτέραις κινήσεσιν γίγνεσθαι πιμελῆς τε ἅμα καὶ σαρκὸς ἁπαλῆς, ὧν μέρος μέν τι διαπνεῖσθαι εἰς τοὐκτὸς, μέρος δ’ ἴσχεσθαι κατὰ τὸ σῶμα, καὶ τοῦτο δριμὺ καὶ δακνῶδες ὑπάρχον, τὴν οἷον ἐλαιώδη διάθεσιν ἐργάζεσθοι. ὥστ’ εἰ καὶ τὸ θεραπεύεσθαι τοὺς κόπους ὑπὸ τοῦ ἐλαίου συμφωνεῖται, τό γε τῆς διαθέσεως τοῦ σώματος ἀμφισβητούμενον οὐδὲν ἐᾷ συλλογίζεσθαι βέβαιον ὑπὲρ ἐλαίου δυνάμεως. οὐ μόνον δὲ ἐπὶ κόπου τοῦτο συμβέβηκεν, ἀλλὰ κᾀπειδὰν ἀλγήματος ἕνεκεν, ἢ δήξεως, ἢ φλεγμονῆς ἔλαιον ποτίζωμεν, ἢ δι’ ἕδρας ἐγχέομεν, ἢ καταντλῶμεν. ἐπὶ μὲν γὰρ τῶν φλεγμαινόντων ἑλκῶν οὐ μόνον, ἀλλὰ μεθ’ ὕδατος αὐτὸ συμμέτρως θερμοῦ καταχέομεν, ὥσπερ ἐν τοῖς κόποις ἅμα τοῖς διὰ τῶν γλυκέων ὑδάτων λουτροῖς. ὁμολογεῖται δ’ οὐδ’ ἐπὶ τῆς φλεγμονῆς, ἡ διάθεσις ὁποία τίς ἐστιν. ἐνιέντες δὲ διὰ τῆς ἕδρας, ἢ ποτίζοντες, οὐδ’ οὖν οὐδὲ τότε γινώσκομεν ἐναργῶς ὁποίῳ τινὶ σώματι προσάγομεν αὐτὸ, πότερα φλέγματος, ἢ χολῆς, ἤ τινων ἄλλων περιττωμάτων μεστῷ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐλαιώδη, ἐργάζεσθοι
Another, however, blames not an abundance of moistures but 486 quality alone, saying that in the more immoderate movements certain colliquations occur of fat together with soft flesh, of which one part is transpired outward, while another part is retained in the body, and this, being pungent and biting, produces the, as it were, oil-like condition. So that even if it is agreed that fatigues are cured by oil, the fact that the condition of the body is disputed allows nothing certain to be inferred about the power of oil. And this happens not only in the case of fatigue, but also whenever, on account of pain, or biting, or inflammation, we give oil to drink, or pour it in through the seat, or foment with it. For in the case of inflamed wounds we pour it over not by itself, but together with moderately warm water, just as in fatigues together with baths of sweet waters. And not even in the case of inflammation is there agreement as to what sort of thing the condition is. And when we inject it through the seat or give it to drink, not even then, indeed, do we know clearly to what sort of body we are applying it, whether to one full of phlegm, or of bile, or of certain other residues.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐλαιώδη (possibly a corruption of ἑλκώδη, 'ulcerous', the Galenic ulcerous type of lassitude; translated as transmitted), ἐργάζεσθοι (read as ἐργάζεσθαι), συντήξεις (colliquations, meltings)
Another blames not a quantity of fluids but 486 a quality alone, saying that in the more immoderate movements certain colliquations take place of fat together with soft flesh, of which one part is dispersed outwards by transpiration, while another part is retained in the body, and this, being pungent and biting, produces the, as it were, oil-like condition. So that, even if it is agreed that lassitudes are treated by oil, the fact that the condition of the body is disputed allows nothing firm to be inferred about the power of oil. And this has happened not only in the case of lassitude, but also whenever we give oil to drink on account of pain, or biting, or inflammation, or inject it through the anus, or pour it over the part. For in the case of inflamed ulcers we pour it on not by itself but with moderately hot water, just as in lassitudes together with baths of sweet waters. Yet not even in the case of inflammation is it agreed what sort of condition it is. And when we inject it through the anus or give it to drink, not even then do we know clearly to what sort of body we are applying it, whether one full of phlegm, or of bile, or of some other residues.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For it is clear that it will not affect the person in the same way when it finds the whole intestine together with the stomach full of viscous phlegm, and when [it finds them full] of bilious residues, or of some other pungent humours, or of much excrement, or when it meets with none of these but with the whole stomach and the intestines perfectly clean, or when, in addition to being clean, this [belly] is also roughened. Most physicians, then, reason very carelessly and negligently about all these matters, not having said in what condition the person is to whom the oil is given, nor even what sort of oil it is; and then some of them assert that it bites and scrapes the inner parts in every case, while others assert that even if there is some biting already present beforehand, it soothes this too. For surely it makes no small difference whether one introduces into a phlegmatic belly the oil which is called, and really is, entirely without salt, or into a clean one that which partakes of a great deal of salt. For both will empty the belly, but by different powers.

Greek (Kühn)
τὸ μὲν γὰρ ἄναλον ἐν φλεγματώδει διὰ τὸν ὄλισθον ὁμοίως 488 μαλάχαις τε καὶ βλίτοις καὶ μόροις· τὸ δὲ ἡλισμένον ἐν καθαρᾷ διὰ τὴν δριμύτητα παραπλησίως ἅλμῃ καὶ θαλάττῃ. καὶ δὴ καὶ ξύσει μὲν τοῦτο, θάτερον δ’ οὐ ξύσει. μὴ τοίνυν μήτε ξύειν ἁπλῶς λέγομεν τοὔλαιον πάντως τἀντὸς, ἢ διαχωρήματα ποιεῖν αἱματώδη, μήτε πραΰνειν δήξεις, ἀλλὰ τὸ μὲν ἁλῶδες ἐν καθαρᾷ ξύειν, τὸ δ’ ἄναλον ἐν φλεγματώδει μὴ ξύειν. Ἐνίοτε δὲ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ἰάσαιτ’ ἂν δήξεις τὸ ἁλμῶδες, ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ μελίκρατον καὶ ἅλμη καὶ γάλακτος ὀῤῥός. συνεξάγονται γὰρ σὺν αὐτοῖς ἐνίοτε ταῦτα τὸν τὴν δῆξιν ἐργαζόμενον χυμὸν, ἰάματά τε γίγνεται δήξεως ὁμοίως τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασιν, τοῖς ἀποπλύνουσί τε καὶ ῥύπτουσιν. ἀλλὰ μετὰ τὴν ἔνεσιν οὐχ ὅπως παύει τὰ τοιαῦτα φάρμακα τὴν δῆξιν εὐθὺς, ἀλλὰ καὶ παροξύνει. τῷ γὰρ τῆς κενώσεως λόγῳ τὴν ἴασιν, οὐ τῷ τῆς ἐπικράσεως ἐργάζεται. κηρωτὴ μέντοι πολλάκις ἐνεθεῖσα διὰ ῥοδίνου καὶ στέατος ἢ πιμελῆς, ἐν τῷ παραχρῆμα τὰς δήξεις ἰάσατο τῷ τῆς ἐπικράσεως λόγῳ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπικράσεως, ἔνεσιν
For the unsalted oil in a phlegmatic belly works through its slipperiness, in the same way as 488 mallows, blites and mulberries; whereas the salted oil in a clean belly works through its pungency, much as brine and sea-water do. And indeed the latter will scrape, while the former will not scrape. Let us therefore say neither, simply, that oil in every case scrapes the inside, or produces bloody stools, nor that it soothes bitings, but rather that the salty oil scrapes in a clean belly, while the unsalted oil in a phlegmatic belly does not scrape. Sometimes, however, the salty oil may cure bitings accidentally, just as melikraton and brine and whey of milk do. For these sometimes carry out together with themselves the humour that produces the biting, and they become remedies for biting in the same way as all the other things that wash away and cleanse. But after the injection such drugs, so far from stopping the biting at once, actually exacerbate it. For they bring about the cure by reason of evacuation, not by reason of tempering. A cerate, however, injected many times, made with rose oil and suet or soft fat, has at once cured the bitings by reason of tempering.

For the unsalted [oil] will loosen a phlegmatic [belly] through its slipperiness, in the same way as 488 mallows, blites and mulberries; the salted [oil] will loosen a clean [belly] through its pungency, similarly to brine and sea-water. And indeed the latter will scrape, while the other will not scrape. Let us therefore say neither that oil simply scrapes the inner parts in every case, or produces bloody evacuations, nor that it soothes bitings. But after the injection such drugs not only fail to stop the biting at once; they even aggravate it. For they effect the cure by way of evacuation, not by way of tempering. A cerate made with rose oil and suet or fat, however, when injected, has often healed bitings on the spot by way of tempering.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λελευκασμένον
Often, too, mixed conditions arise from dyscrasia together with biting, in which we are accustomed to inject oil of dill or oil of rue, having melted goose fat into it; if this is not available, then at least 490 that of hens; and if not this either, one of the others. And if none of these is at hand, we mix in some non-biting wax—this is what I call the washed wax—or indeed, by Zeus, the oil itself, whitened, injected on its own; for this too is analogous to non-biting wax. Rash, then, and undiscriminating is the assertion both of those who have declared every oil to be biting on every occasion and of those who have declared it soothing. For the briny kind is biting, while the saltless and sweet kind is soothing, as far as depends on itself; for even this might sometimes seem, owing to the conditions to which it is applied, no longer to be such. For each of the tempering drugs, when it becomes stronger than the biting humours, blunts their biting; but if a small amount of it is mixed with humours stronger in their powers or greater in quantity, it is corrupted along with them and nourishes the cacochymia, whence one must also evacuate at once those who are exacerbated by the tempering remedies, as being filled with much corruption that is hard to move.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἢ πολλαῖς ὀλίγον ἀναμιχθῇ, δυσκινήτου, ἀνάλογον γὰρ ἐστιν ἀδήκτῳ κηρῷ καὶ τοῦτο
Often, too, mixed conditions arise from bad mixture and biting together; in these we are accustomed to pour in oil of dill or oil of rue, having first melted goose fat into it; if this is not at hand, then 490 at any rate fowl's fat; and if not even this, one of the others. When none of these is available, we mix in some unbiting wax (I call so the washed wax), or indeed, by Zeus, the oil itself, the whitened kind, poured in. For this too is analogous to unbiting wax. Rash, then, and undiscriminating is both the pronouncement of those who have declared all oil biting on every occasion and that of those who have declared it soothing. For the briny oil is biting, while the unsalted and sweet oil is soothing, so far as lies in itself. For this too might sometimes seem, depending on the conditions to which it is applied, to be no longer of this character. For each of the tempering drugs, when it becomes stronger than the biting humours, blunts their biting. But if it is mixed in, in small quantity, with stronger powers, or with many, it is corrupted along with them and nourishes the bad humours; hence those who are exacerbated by the tempering remedies must also be evacuated at once, as being filled with much corruption that is hard to move.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κνισοῦται, κερχνώδους, στόμαχον
491 Thus, then, milk too often, as soon as it has been taken, turns sour in some people and nidorous in others, before, having been well concocted, it can temper the corruption. And in these people one must first evacuate the greater part of the cacochymia and only then administer both groats and ptisane and milk and all the other things which, when well concocted, contribute to euchymia; one must not, however, suppose, so far as this is concerned, that all of them possess either worse or contrary powers, nor deliberately pass over in one's discussions one of the two things that happen, just as Archidamus, in the case of oil, mentioned the eyes that are bitten and the rasping throat and the belly that is scraped, but that sweet oil, when drunk, has often at once cured even a stomach that was being strongly bitten, and when injected has soothed the bitings throughout the whole belly, and when smeared on has stopped those arising on the skin from thapsia and nettle and squill—this he no longer mentioned.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κνισοῦται, κερχνώδους, ὅσον ἐπὶ τούτῳ, στόμαχον
491 In the same way, then, milk too often, as soon as it has been administered, turns sour in some people and becomes nidorous in others, before, having been well concocted, it can temper the corruption. In these cases one must first evacuate the bulk of the bad humours, and only then administer groats and ptisan and milk and the other things which, when well concocted, contribute to good humours. One must not, however, suppose that all these, so far as depends on themselves, possess either the worse or the contrary powers, nor in one's account deliberately pass over one of the two sets of things that happen, as Archidamus did in the case of oil. He mentioned the eyes that are bitten, the rasping throat and the scraped belly, but he no longer mentioned that sweet oil, when drunk, has often at once healed even a stomach that was strongly bitten, that when injected it has soothed the bitings throughout the whole belly, and that when smeared on it has stopped those arising on the skin from thapsia, nettle and squill.
