K11.491-492K. XI 491–492, ch. 2.13 difficulty high (3.35/100; thin 5, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ἐγὼ δ’ οὐκ ἀμφισβητῶ μὲν οὐδ’ αὐτὸς, οὐδ’ ἀπιστῶ τοῖς φάσκουσιν ἐν ἅπασι σχεδὸν τοῖς οὖσι πολλὰς καὶ διαφερούσας περιέχεσθαι δυνάμεις, ἀλλὰ καὶ δείξειν 492 ἐπαγγέλλομαι τοῦτο κατὰ τὸν ἑξῆς λόγον. οὐ μὴν τούτου γε ἕνεκα καὶ τὴν ἐν ἑκάστῳ κρατοῦσαν ἄγνωστον εἶναί φημι. διοριζομένῳ δέ σοι τὸ καθ’ αὑτὸ τοῦ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ἐπιγνωσθήσεται σαφῶς ἅπαντα. θέασαι γὰρ ὡδί πως ἑκάτερον ἐπὶ παραδειγμάτων, ὑπόδειγμα θέμενος ἐν τῷ λόγῳ δύο τινὰς ἀνθρώπους δακνομένους μὲν ὁμοίως τὸ τῆς γαστρὸς στόμα, προσφερομένους δὲ χυλὸν πτισάνης ἢ χόνδρον ἢ ἄρτον πλυτὸν, εἶτα τῷ μὲν ἑτέρῳ παυομένην τὴν δῆξιν, αὐξανομένην δὲ θατέρῳ, πότερον οὖν ὅσον ἐφ’ ἑαυτῷ δακνώδης ἐστὶν ὁ χυλὸς τῆς πτισάνης, ἢ ὁ χόνδρος, ἢ ὁ ἄρτος, κατὰ δέ τι συμβεβηκὸς ἐπὶ θατέρου τῶν ἀνθρώπων οὐχ ὅπως ηὔξησεν, ἀλλὰ παρηγόρησεν τὴν δῆξιν; ἢ παρηγόρησεν μὲν ὅσον ἐφ’ ἑαυτῷ, κατὰ δέ τι συμβεβηκὸς ἐπὶ θατέρου παρώξυνεν; ἐγὼ μὲν ἐπικεραστικὸν ὑπάρχοντα κατά γε τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν φημὶ τὸν χυλὸν τῆς πτισάνης, ἢ τὸν χόνδρον, ἢ τὸν ἄρτον, ἢ νὴ Δία τὸ γάλα, παροξύνεσθαι δὲ θατέρῳ τῶν ἀνθρώπων τὴν δῆξιν, τῷ φθάσαι νικηθῆναι πρὸς τῆς διαφθορᾶς.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χόνδρον, ἐπικεραστικὸν
I myself do not dispute it either, nor do I disbelieve those who assert that in nearly all existing things many and differing powers are contained; indeed I promise to demonstrate 492 this in the discourse that follows. Yet not on this account do I say that the power which prevails in each thing is unknowable. If you distinguish what is per se from what is per accidens, everything will be clearly recognized. For consider each case in some such way as this by means of examples, positing in the argument as an illustration two persons who are bitten alike at the mouth of the stomach and who take the juice of ptisane or spelt-groats or washed bread, and then for the one the biting ceases, while for the other it increases. Is it, then, that so far as depends on itself the juice of ptisane, or the spelt-groats, or the bread, is biting, but by some accident in the case of one of the two men it not only did not increase but even soothed the biting? Or did it soothe so far as depends on itself, but by some accident in the case of the other man exacerbate it? For my part, I say that the juice of ptisane, or the spelt-groats, or the bread, or, by Zeus, milk, being tempering in its own nature, the biting is exacerbated in the other of the two men because it has first been overcome by the corruption.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπικεραστικὸν, τῷ φθάσαι νικηθῆναι, ὑπόδειγμα
I myself neither dispute nor disbelieve those who say that in almost all existing things many and differing powers are contained; on the contrary, I even undertake to demonstrate 492 this in the discussion that follows. Not, however, on this account do I say that the power which prevails in each thing is also unknowable. But if you distinguish what is so in itself from what is so by accident, everything will be clearly recognised. For consider each of the two in some such way as this, by means of examples, setting up as an illustration in the argument two men who suffer biting alike at the mouth of the stomach, and who take the juice of ptisane, or groats (chondros), or washed bread; then in the one the biting ceases, while in the other it increases. Is, then, the juice of ptisane, or the groats, or the bread, biting so far as lies in itself, but by some accident, in the case of the other of the two men, not only did not increase the biting but actually soothed it? Or did it soothe so far as lies in itself, but by some accident, in the case of the other, exacerbate it? I for my part say that the juice of ptisane, or the groats, or the bread, or, by Zeus, the milk, is tempering by its own nature, and that the biting is exacerbated in the other of the two men because the food has first been overcome by the corruption.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And thus I know that nearly all the 493 ancient physicians too held, just as also the best of the more recent ones; though there might perhaps be someone who escapes my notice. For nothing is beyond the daring of some of the more recent physicians, so that they would declare that groats, or bread, or ptisan by their own nature increase the corruptions, but in some cases soothe them by some accident. But he who says these things has no demonstration to offer for his assertion. We, however, say that if ptisan were by nature of bad humour, it would never have soothed bitings. But in fact it is often seen to soothe, both when taken from above and when injected through the seat; from which it is clear that in its own nature it is of good humour, but when at times it is corrupted together with the humours that produce the bitings, it increases the symptom. Perhaps, then, someone might ask us whether none of the things of bad humour has ever cured a biting. I for my part know that this happens not infrequently; but I say that such drugs cure the bitings not by tempering, but by carrying out along with themselves that which bites.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χόνδρον
Thus too, I know, almost all the 493 ancient physicians understood it, as do the best of the more recent ones as well, though there may perhaps be someone who escapes my notice. For nothing is too audacious for some of the more recent physicians, so that they declare that groats, or bread, or ptisane increases the corruptions by its own nature, yet in some people soothes them by some accident. But whoever says this has no demonstration to offer for his pronouncement. We, however, say that if ptisane were by nature productive of bad humours, it would never soothe bitings. Yet in fact it is seen, whether taken from above or injected through the anus, often soothing, from which it is clear that in its own nature it is productive of good humours; but when it is sometimes corrupted together with the humours that produce the bitings, it increases the symptom. Perhaps, then, someone might ask us whether none of the things productive of bad humours has ever cured a biting. For my part I know that this happens not infrequently; but I say that such drugs cure the bitings not by tempering them, but by carrying out along with themselves what is biting.

And for this reason [I say that they] both immediately and until 494 such time as they have been evacuated bite the person and leave behind a certain rough sensation in the parts, one that needs another drug — not so, however, the well-humoured and sweet-humoured ones. For these soothe both immediately and without producing any evacuation, and they bring on no rough sensation; quite the contrary, they are curative of rough sensations. At any rate, chondros and milk and ptisane we both give to eat in such conditions and inject through the seat, once we have washed away the troubling matter either by means of honey or some other such thing. And at this juncture fat too becomes useful to us, and sweet cerate and sweet oil. Consider that both now and throughout the following discussion I mean all sweet things, both those prepared without salt and those that have been washed. First, whether it soothes immediately or not; second, whether the distressing matters are evacuated along with it or not; 495 third, whether it leaves behind some roughness in the bodies or not; and fourth, whether it would not appear beneficial to those who are in such a condition. For that which neither soothes the bitings immediately, but is rather excreted with greater vehemence, nor remains within and heals, and which leaves behind some painful roughness in the bodies and exacerbates this if it is applied a second time, is erosive and biting by nature, though it sometimes heals bitings by accident; whereas that which is soothing even immediately and provokes no excretion, nor roughens while it remains, and becomes a remedy for the parts that have been roughened, is itself beneficial by nature, but there are times when it becomes biting through the cacochymia which alters and corrupts it.

First, whether it soothes at once or not; second, whether the troublesome matters are evacuated together with it or not; 495 third, whether it leaves behind some roughness about the bodies or not; and fourth, whether it should fail to appear wholesome to those who are in this condition. For that which neither soothes the bitings at once, but is excreted with greater violence, nor heals by remaining within, and which leaves behind about the bodies a distressing roughness and aggravates it if it is applied a second time, is erosive and biting by nature, but by accident sometimes heals bitings. That which is soothing at once and provokes no excretion, does not roughen while it remains, and becomes a remedy for what has been roughened, is itself wholesome by nature, but sometimes becomes biting through the bad humours that alter and corrupt it.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατάῤῥοντας, τράγον, χόνδρον
These things have been said by me, and the argument has taught at once the occasion or the manner of use of each of the two kinds of drugs and at once the discovery of their power, and it has also demonstrated that the juice of ptisane, and chondros, and tragos, and milk, and washed bread belong to the things of good humour, and indeed also that soft fat and hard fat, and sweet oil 496 and sweet wax, are of the nature of the soothing agents. And sweet wax would be both that which is such by nature—since some, at least, are bitter to those who taste them—and that which, in addition to being such, has also been washed in good water. One must wash not only wax but also pitch and resin, and oil and anything else whatsoever of this kind that does not mix with water, first warming it moderately, then pouring it down into a broad and large vessel containing in itself a quantity of water that is at once most pure and most devoid of quality. For in this the thing being washed, whatever it may be, deposits its own pungency. And one must also rub it up and beat and crush and knead it with the hands sufficiently, so that every part of the drug being washed may come into contact with the water; and having done this, one must pour off the first water, and, warming the thing being washed once more, throw it into other water, likewise pure; and one must beat it up again for as long as possible, until it deposits its own quality in the water.

These things have been said by me, and the argument has at once taught the right moment or the manner of use of each of the two kinds of drugs, and at once the discovery of the power. It has also shown that the juice of ptisane, chondros, tragos, milk and washed bread belong among the things of good juice, and indeed that fat and suet, sweet oil 496 and sweet wax are of the nature of soothing things. For into this the thing being washed, whatever it may be, deposits its own pungency. One must also rub it, beat it, squeeze it and knead it with the hands sufficiently, so that every part of the drug being washed comes into contact with the water. Having done this, one must pour off the first water, and, warming the thing being washed again, throw it into other water that is likewise clean, and one must beat it up again for as long as possible, until it deposits its own quality in the water.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐφίστηται, ἐποχούμενον
And it is possible for you to discern this also by tasting; then to do this a third and 497 fourth time and many times in succession, until the water no longer receives any quality at all from the drug being washed. In this way, then, one should wash all things that can be heated and melted. But if something is stony and hard, like cadmia and scale of copper and burnt copper, one should break those down thoroughly into fine pieces and grind them for a very long time in spring water that is as pure as possible, pouring off the previous water many times, until nothing settles on top when the water stands still and it becomes without any quality of the drug being ground; but so long as either something of its quality is perceptible to those tasting, or something settles on top, up to that point one should continue both grinding and pouring off. For if one washes in this way any of the things that can be washed, it becomes non-biting. And indeed oil appears in this process to become not only non-biting but also whiter. But one must not beat it up vigorously in the same way as pitch and wax and resin, so that its mixture with the water does not become hard to separate; rather, having let it stand still, one should easily take it up as it floats on top.

You can also discern this by tasting. Then do this a third and a 497 fourth time, and many times in succession, until the water no longer receives any quality from the drug being washed. In this way, then, one should wash everything that can be heated and made to flow. But if something is stony and hard, such as cadmia, flake of copper and burnt copper, one should break these up thoroughly into fine pieces and grind them for a very long time in spring water that is as pure as possible, often pouring off the earlier water, until nothing any longer forms on the surface when the water has settled, and the water becomes without quality from the drug being ground. As long as either something of its quality is apparent to those who taste it, or something forms on the surface, for so long one should keep grinding and pouring off. One must not, however, beat it vigorously in the same way as pitch, wax and resin, so that its mixture with the water may not become hard to separate. Rather, once one has let it stand still, it is easy to take it off as it floats on top.

There are also other methods of whitening oil among those who have written the books 498 on preparations and those on materia medica, as also the ones in Dioscorides, in the first book. But the majority of those methods, by consuming the watery and the yellowish-red part of the oil, render what remains white, whereas the method by washing does so by, as it were, scouring it clean and wiping it off. Concerning the condition of washed drugs and of those that are whitened, more will be said again later; but I return to the matter at hand. Oil, if it is neither the so-called omphacine (for that at any rate is astringent), nor prepared with salt, nor yet fresh and, as it were, still seething, and if in addition to all these it has been carefully washed, will produce no biting either when drunk or when injected. And if it is roughening to the pharynx, this at least would be nothing towards its being biting. For everything that is biting and pungent is also roughening, but certainly not everything roughening is biting and pungent. For only in this way would oil have been demonstrated to be biting, namely if everything roughening had been agreed to be biting.

There are also other methods of whitening oil, set out by those who have written both the 498 books on preparations and those on materia medica, such as those in Dioscorides too, in the first [book]. But most of those methods, by using up the watery and the tawny part of the oil, render the remainder white, whereas the method by washing does so by, as it were, cleansing it of dirt and wiping it clean. Concerning the condition of drugs that can be washed and whitened, more will be said later. But I return to the matter at hand. And if it is roughening to the throat, this would contribute nothing toward its being biting. For everything that bites and is pungent is also roughening, but not everything roughening is biting and pungent. For only in this way would oil have been shown to be biting, if it had been agreed that everything roughening is biting.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σφάλματος
So here too there comes in again 499 the fallacy arising from the inversion of the conditional, this a small and paltry sort of error. For it is agreed that everything biting is throat-roughening; and it is agreed also that oil is throat-roughening. But from these assumptions it is not concluded that oil is biting, whether we make the premises categorical or hypothetical, because neither is anything concluded from two universal affirmatives in the second figure, nor is the conditional necessarily true. In the categorical syllogism the two premises will be these: everything biting is throat-roughening, all oil is throat-roughening; and from these, once granted, nothing will be concluded. As for the hypothetical premise, which Chrysippus and his school call a conditional proposition, we have no way of taking it as true either from perception or from logical consequence.

Here, then, there comes in once again 499 the error arising from the conversion of the conditional, a small and paltry sort of error. For it is agreed that everything that bites is roughening, and it is agreed too that oil is roughening. But from these assumptions it is not concluded that oil is biting, whether we make the premises categorical or hypothetical, because nothing is concluded from two universal affirmatives in the second figure, and the conditional is not necessarily true. In the categorical syllogism the two premises will be these: everything that bites is roughening; all oil is roughening. When these are granted, nothing will be concluded from them. And the hypothetical premise, which Chrysippus and his school call a conditional proposition, we cannot take as true either from perception or from logical consequence.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὐ μὲν γὰρ, ἀντιστρέφει, ἀναστρέφει
For it is not the case that, either through perception or from perception, or however one may wish to name it, axioms of the following sort have been obtained—'everything biting is also throat-roughening', and 'if something is biting, this is also throat-roughening'—nor, if everything biting is also throat-roughening, is everything throat-roughening 500 thereby also biting; nor from 'everything, if it is biting, is also throat-roughening' does it follow that 'everything, if it is throat-roughening, is also biting'. Rather, the proposition 'if not throat-roughening, then not biting either' is true, for it converts; but not 'if something is throat-roughening, it is forthwith also biting', for this inverts. And it has been shown in the logical methods that arguments which convert with true arguments are true, whereas those which invert are no longer in every case true. If, then, it is not necessarily true that, if something is throat-roughening, this is forthwith also biting, neither would oil be shown to be biting from its being throat-roughening. For only if it had been agreed that everything throat-roughening is biting would such a conditional have been found true—'if oil is throat-roughening, it is also biting'—so that, when a co-assumption of the following kind has been added to this argument, 'but indeed oil is throat-roughening', the conclusion follows, according to the first indemonstrable, as follows: 'therefore oil is biting'.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐ μὲν γὰρ (the negation sits awkwardly with the sense; possibly corrupt), ἀντιστρέφει (contraposition, as distinct from ἀναστρέφει, simple reversal), ἵνα ἕπηται ... ἕπεται (doubled verb; syntax apparently disturbed), προσλήψεως
For it is not [?] through perception, or from perception, or however one may wish to name it, that propositions of this kind have been obtained: 'everything biting is also roughening', and 'if something is biting, this is also roughening'. Yet neither, if everything biting is roughening, does it follow that everything roughening 500 is thereby biting; nor from 'everything, if it is biting, is also roughening' does there follow 'everything, if it is roughening, is also biting'. Rather, 'if it is not roughening, neither is it biting' is true, for it converts by contraposition (ἀντιστρέφει); but not 'if something is roughening, it is straightway also biting', for this is a mere reversal (ἀναστρέφει). It has been shown in the logical methods that statements which are contrapositives of true statements are true, whereas those which are reversals are no longer true in every case. If, then, it is not of necessity true that if something is roughening it is straightway also biting, neither could oil be shown to be biting from its being roughening. For only if it had been agreed that everything roughening is biting would a conditional of this sort have been found true, 'if oil is roughening, it is also biting'. Then, when an additional assumption (πρόσληψις) of this kind is made to this statement, 'but oil is roughening', the conclusion follows, according to the first undemonstrated [argument], as follows: 'therefore oil is biting'.

Yet neither, if everything biting is roughening, does it follow that everything roughening [500] is thereby biting; nor from 'everything, if it is biting, is also roughening' does there follow 'everything, if it is roughening, is also biting'.

Greek (Kühn)
ἀλλὰ γὰρ οὐχ οὕτως ἐπιχειρητέον 501 ἦν αὐτοῖς, ὡς εἴ τι δακνῶδες, εὐθύς ἐστι τοῦτο καὶ κερχνῶδες, ἀλλ’ ὅτι μόνα τὰ δακνώδη κερχνώδη· εἴη γὰρ ἂν οὕτω τὰ κερχνώδη πάντα δακνώδη. τοῦτο δ’ αὐτὸ, τὸ μόνα τὰ δακνώδη κερχνώδη φαίνεσθαι, παρά τε τὴν αἴσθησιν εἴρηται. φαίνεται γὰρ καὶ τὰ λιπαρὰ κερχνώδη καὶ τὸ ζητούμενον ἑτοίμως εἴληπται, μήτ’ ἐξ αἰσθήσεως μήτ’ ἐξ ἀποδείξεως πιστωθέν. ἔτι τε πρὸς τούτοις ἀγνοούντων ἐστὶν ἀνθρώπων, ὁποία τις ἡ τοῦ κέρχνειν ἐστὶ διάθεσις, ἣν ἴσως ἄμεινον αὐτοῖς ἐξηγήσασθαι. Συμβαίνει δὲ τὸ κέρχνειν ἀνωμαλίας τινὸς ἐν τῇ φάρυγγι συνισταμένης παρὰ φύσιν. εἰθισμένον γὰρ τὸ πνεῦμα διὰ καθαρᾶς καὶ λείας ἀεὶ φέρεσθαι τῆς ὁδοῦ, ἐπειδὰν προσκόπτῃ τισὶν ἐξοχαῖς κατ’ αὐτὴν, κλόνον τέ τινα καὶ οἷον ἀνώμαλον σεισμὸν ἐργάζεται κατὰ τὴν φάρυγγα, καὶ οὕτως ἐρεθίζεται πρὸς βῆχα τὸ ζῶον. ὥσπερ γὰρ καὶ ἄλλα πολλὰ σωτηρίας ἕνεκα τοῖς ζώοις ἡ φύσις ἐναπειργάσατο σύμφυτα πάθη, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ πταρμὸν καὶ βῆχα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κέρχνειν
But in fact they ought not to have argued 501 in this way, that if something is biting, this is straightaway also throat-roughening, but rather that only biting things are throat-roughening; for in that way all throat-roughening things would be biting. But this very claim, that only biting things appear throat-roughening, is both stated contrary to sense-perception—for oily things too appear throat-roughening—and the point at issue has been readily assumed, confirmed neither from perception nor from demonstration. And further, in addition to these things, it is the mark of men who are ignorant of what sort of condition that of throat-roughness (κέρχνειν) is, which it is perhaps better to explain to them. Throat-roughness comes about when some unevenness contrary to nature is formed in the throat. For the pneuma, being accustomed always to be carried along a clean and smooth path, when it strikes against certain protrusions along it, produces a certain agitation and, as it were, an uneven shaking in the throat, and in this way the animal is provoked to cough. For just as nature has also implanted in animals many other innate affections for the sake of preservation, in the same way it has also implanted sneezing and cough.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κερχνώδης, κέρχνειν, κλόνον, ἐναπειργάσατο
But in fact they ought not to have argued in this way, 501 that if something is biting, it is at once also roughening, but rather that only biting things are roughening; for in that way all roughening things would be biting. Yet this very claim, that only biting things appear roughening, is made contrary to perception (for oily things too appear roughening), and the point at issue has been readily assumed, confirmed neither from perception nor from demonstration. And further, besides these things, it is characteristic of people who do not know what sort of condition being roughened is, which it is perhaps better to explain to them. Being roughened occurs when some unevenness forms in the throat contrary to nature. For the pneuma, being accustomed always to travel through a clean and smooth passage, whenever it strikes against certain projections in it, produces a kind of agitation and, as it were, an uneven shaking in the throat, and in this way the animal is provoked to cough. For just as nature has wrought into animals many other innate affections for the sake of their preservation, in the same way she has also wrought sneezing and cough.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὐ μὴν οὐ διΐσταταί γε, διΐσταται, στερὸν
sneezing in order to thrust out the things that irritate and distress in the 502 nostrils, and cough in order to thrust out those in the throat. Such a condition is twofold: either the membrane itself along the artery is dried out and roughened, or some uneven body is carried within it, whether liquid in its nature or even solid. For this reason, even if some small particle of bread, or of some other of our foods, should at some time slip, against our will, into the passage of the breath, we are compelled to cough; and if, while we are drinking, some of the drink flows aside all at once, it is similarly irritated and calls forth a cough. For some portion of what is drunk is always filtered through along the rough artery into the lung, but so small that it is at once snatched up by the organs and readily distributed in every direction. It does not, however, by any means lodge in a mass (διΐσταται) anywhere in the passages of the breath, nor is the animal irritated to cough. But whenever either more of this, or some other such liquid, or a solid body is carried into the rough artery, from then on it is necessary to cough, until what distresses is cast out by the vehemence of the breath.

sneezing for the sake of thrusting out the things in the 502 nostrils that irritate and distress them, and coughing for the sake of thrusting out those in the throat. Such a condition is twofold: either the membrane along the artery is itself being dried up and roughened, or some uneven body is being carried in it, whether liquid or solid in nature. For this reason, if some small particle of bread or of some other food ever slips, against our will, into the passage of the breath, we are compelled to cough; and if, while we are drinking, some of the drink flows aside in a mass, it is likewise irritated and calls forth a cough. For some of what is drunk always filters through the rough artery into the lung, but so little that it is at once snatched up by the organs and readily distributed in every direction. It does not, however, stand apart in a mass anywhere in the passages of the breath, nor is the animal provoked to coughing. But whenever either this in a larger quantity, or some other such liquid, or a solid body is carried into the rough artery, it is from then on necessary to cough, until what is causing the distress is thrown out by the violence of the breath.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔσωθεν δὲ προσπίπτει, κέρχνον
Something analogous to this occurs also when the breath strikes against the very membrane lining the inside of the windpipe once it has become roughened, 503 except that this condition is different from the one mentioned before. For this one takes away and scrapes off something of the membrane of the windpipe, whereas in the former condition something falls upon it on the inside. What is common to both is the unevenness of the passage of the breath. These matters have been discussed at greater length in the books on the Causes of Symptoms. But for the present it suffices to know that all biting things produce the unevenness and the hoarseness (kerchnos) by roughening the throat, while oil-like and oily things do so by being plastered around it. For whatever falls upon the coat of the windpipe contrary to nature and is plastered on so as to be hard to detach becomes unfamiliar to the organs and to the breath carried through them, and distressing to the animal. And I am not now saying this as though most oils were not also biting in their quality.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔσωθεν, αἰτίας, κέρχνον, ἐμπλάσσεταί
Something analogous to this also happens when the breath strikes against the membrane itself on the inside of the windpipe (ἀρτηρία) after it has been roughened 503; this condition, however, is different from the one described before. For in this one something is taken away and scraped off from the membrane of the windpipe, whereas in the former condition something falls upon it on the inside. What is common to both is the unevenness of the passage of the breath. These matters have been discussed more fully in the [books on the] Causes of Symptoms. Greek (Kühn)
τά τε γὰρ ἁλῶν μετέχοντα καὶ τὰ παντάπασι νέα δακνώδη πάντως ἐστὶν, οὐ διὰ τὴν ἰδίαν αὐτῶν οὐσίαν, ἀλλ’ ὅτι πᾶσι τοῖς ἐκ τῶν φυτῶν χυλοῖς ἐμφέρεται περίττωμα διττὸν, ὡς καὶ Θεόφραστος μὲν ἱκανῶς ἐπέδειξε καὶ ἡμεῖς δ’ ἐν τοῖς ἑξῆς ἐπιδείξομεν, ἕτερον μὲν τὸ παχύτερον ﻿ 504 καὶ οἷον γεωδέστερον, ἕτερον δὲ τὸ λεπτότερον καὶ ὑδατωδέστερον. ἔστι δὲ καὶ κατὰ τοὔλαιον ταῦτ’ ἄμφω χρόνου δεόμενα πρὸς σύμπεψίν τε καὶ διάκρισιν, ἀλλ’ ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ τὸ μὲν ὑφίσταται, τὸ δ’ ἐκδαπανᾶται. καὶ πρότερόν γε τὸ γεῶδες καὶ παχύτερον ὑφίσταται τὸ καλούμενον ἀμόργη, θάτερον δὲ τὸ λεπτότερον καὶ οἷον ὑδατωδέστερον, ἔστι πως καὶ τοῦτο σύνθετον, ἐξ ἀερώδους τέ τινος οὐσίας καὶ λεπτῆς ὀῤῥώδους. Τὸ δ’ ὀῤῥῶδες τοῦτο πυῤῥὸν μὲν τὴν χρόαν ἐστὶ, τοῦ δ’ ὑφισταμένου καὶ παχέος καὶ γεώδους ἀπομέμακται τὴν ποιότητα. αὐτὸ δὲ τοὔλαιον ἄδηκτόν τε καὶ λευκόν ἐστι κατά γε τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν, ὅθεν ἐπειδὰν ἀποχωρισθῇ τὸ λεπτὸν τοῦτο περίττωμα, λευκότερόν τε καὶ γλυκύτερον ἑαυτοῦ γίγνεται. ἀποχωρίζεται δὲ τρό ποις πολλοῖς. ἢ γὰρ ἐν ἡλίῳ θερμῷ θέντες ἀγγεῖον εὐρύστομον ἐλαίου μεστὸν, αἴροντες κόγχαις ἐξ αὐτοῦ πολλάκις ἄνωθεν καταράττουσιν, ἢ θερμὴν ὑποτιθέασιν τέφραν τοῖς περιέχουσιν ἀγγείοις τοὔλαιον, ἡλιοειδῆ θερμασίαν ὡς ἔνι μάλιστα μιμούμενοι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπομέμακται, καταράττουσιν
For those that contain salt and those that are altogether new are in every case biting, not because of their own proper substance, but because in all the juices derived from plants there is carried along a twofold residue, as Theophrastus has sufficiently demonstrated and as we too shall demonstrate in what follows: the one the thicker 504 and, as it were, more earthy, the other the thinner and more watery. In oil too both of these are present, requiring time for concoction and separation; but in the course of time the one settles as sediment, the other is consumed away. And first the earthy and thicker part settles, what is called amorge; the other, the thinner and, as it were, more watery part, is itself also in a way composite, consisting of a certain airy substance and a thin serous one. This serous part is tawny in colour, and has taken on by impression the quality of the thick and earthy part that settles. The oil itself, however, is non-biting and white according to its own nature; whence, when this thin residue has been separated off, it becomes whiter and sweeter than it was. It is separated off in many ways. For either, having set a wide-mouthed vessel full of oil in the hot sun, they repeatedly lift it out of it with shells and dash it down from above, or they place hot ash beneath the vessels that contain the oil, imitating as far as possible a sun-like warmth.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀῤῥώδους, ὀῤῥῶδες, πυῤῥὸν, κόγχαις, καταράττουσιν, ἀπομέμακται
For those that contain salt, and those that are quite new, are altogether biting. This is not because of their own proper substance, but because in all the juices from plants a twofold residue is carried along, as Theophrastus has sufficiently shown and as we too shall show in what follows: the one thicker 504 and, as it were, more earthy, the other thinner and more watery. Both of these are present in oil too, and they require time for concoction and separation; but in time the one settles and the other is consumed. First the earthy and thicker one settles, the so-called amurca (ἀμόργη). The other, the thinner and, as it were, more watery one, is itself in a way also compound, made up of a certain airy substance and a thin whey-like one. This whey-like part is tawny in colour, and it has taken on the impress of the quality of the settling, thick and earthy part. But the oil itself is non-biting and white in its own nature. Hence, once this thin residue has been separated off, it becomes whiter and sweeter than it was. It is separated off in many ways. Either they set a wide-mouthed vessel full of oil in the hot sun, take it up with shells and repeatedly dash it down from above, or they put hot ash under the vessels containing the oil, imitating as closely as possible the sun-like warmth.

505 For they guard against the greater heat, which alters it in quality and readily gives it a burnt, greasy smell; or they set olive-stones or grain around it in a circle, or they add certain drugs to the vessels themselves; in a word, they draw out and consume the watery part and preserve what remains. If, then, you take oil that is sweet and white in this way, which from the beginning had no share of salt at all, and in addition also wash it, as was said before, this is for you the least biting of all drugs. And indeed we have often given oil of this kind to certain people to drink, and have ourselves taken it for the sake of trial, and it was entirely non-biting in the intestine and least roughening to the throat, and when poured into the eyes it brought on a slight sensation, troubling them by its strangeness and unfamiliarity alone rather than by its pungency. Oil of this sort, then, being oil in the strict sense, moistens and softens everything, being neither extremely cold, as has been said, nor hot when applied.

505 For they guard against the greater heat, which alters it in quality and readily makes it smell burnt; or they set olive stones or grain in a circle around it, or they also throw certain drugs into the vessels themselves. But that oil which also has a share of salt necessarily has something of biting as well; yet I should be surprised if anyone 506 puts in so much salt that it becomes stronger than the oil-like and oily substance, and that merely in order that someone may prove Archidamus right when he says that the skin is scorched in those who are rubbed with oil—since indeed I know that this too is often done, both for certain other purposes and for podagra and arthritis. For some people rub the affected parts with finely ground salt together with a little oil, in the intervals obviously and not during the paroxysms, in order to dry up and disperse all the superfluity and to instil a certain good condition and strength into the weak parts. But in this case the salt predominates, whereas in oil it is predominated over. And one ought to compare the person who is anointed and rubbed with things that are soaked in oil rather than with things that are boiled in it, since we are surely not going to boil the man down in boiling oil, but to anoint him with well-tempered oil. For it would have been better, as I have often said before as well, to look at the thing itself as it happens, since it is perceptible and perfectly clear. For there is surely no need of examples from outside in matters that can be known in this way.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βυρσῶν
But if indeed 507 at some time the matter under investigation should also be unclear, so as to require proof from other things, not even then should one set down as testimony of the power of the thing under investigation things that are most remote and most dissimilar, passing over what lies near at hand. It is surely evident and clear to all that rubbing with oil is far less fatiguing than dry rubbing and softens bodies, rather holding together and keeping in whatever is of a nature to flow off from us outward, not dispersing and drying it. And this Diocles himself also conceded, when he declared oil to be clogging of the pores, restraining of what flows off, and softening of hides. But if what is apparent did not suffice for him, and for this reason he thought it better to adduce to his argument some piece of external evidence, he ought to have mentioned things that are soaked in cold oil, not things that are boiled down in boiling oil. Just as in such matters he passes over what is apparent, so too does he in asserting that rubbing in oil becomes uneven because the hands slip.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
But if 507 ever the thing under investigation should be unclear, so as to need proof from other things, not even then should one set down the most remote and most dissimilar testimonies to the power of the thing under investigation, passing over what lies close at hand. It is surely evident and clear to all that rubbing with oil is much less fatiguing than dry rubbing, and that it softens bodies, holding together and shutting in, rather than dispersing and drying, whatever naturally flows off from us to the outside. Diocles himself also agreed with this, declaring that oil is clogging of the pores, retentive of what flows off, and softening of hides. But if what appears was not sufficient for him, and for this reason he thought it better to bring some external proof into the argument, he ought to have mentioned things soaked through in cold oil, not things boiled down in boiling oil. Just as in such matters he passes over what appears, so too in asserting that rubbing in oil becomes uneven, as the hands slip.

Greek (Kühn)
αὐτὸ γὰρ δὴ τοὐναντίον ἅπαντες ἐναργῶς αἰσθανόμεθα, τῆς μὲν δι’ 508 ἐλαίου τρίψεως ὁμαλοῦς γιγνομένης, ἀνωμάλου δὲ τῆς ξηρᾶς. συνεπάγονται γὰρ ἐν ταῖς ξηραῖς τρίψεσιν αἱ χεῖρες τὸ δέρμα καὶ οἷον διπλοῦσί τε καὶ ἐπιπτύσσουσιν ἐν αὐτῷ βεβαίως τε καὶ παρὰ φύσιν. εἶθ’ ὅταν μηκέτι τὸ πρότερον οἷον ἐνδίπλωμα παραπτομέναις ταῖς χερσὶν ἕπεσθαι δύνηται, τουτὶ μὲν ἀνατρέχει καὶ ἀνασπᾶται πρὸς τὸ συνεχὲς, αἱ χεῖρες δ’ ἀθρόως ἐφ’ ἕτερον τοῦ δέρματος μετέρχονται μόριον, ὑπερβαίνουσαι τὸ κάτωθεν μέρος τῆς ἐνδιπλώσεως καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ σκληρᾶς καὶ ἀνωμάλου καὶ κοπώδους τῆς τοιαύτης τρίψεως αἰσθανόμεθα· σκηρᾶς μὲν, ὅτι τε τείνεται βιαίως καὶ θλᾶται τὸ δέρμα, διὰ δὲ τὰ αὐτὰ καὶ κοπώδους, ἀνωμάλου μὲν διότι μὴ παντὶ μορίῳ τοῦ δέρματος αἱ τρίβουσαι χεῖρες ἐπιπίπτουσιν, ἀλλ’ ὑπερβαίνουσιν ἀεὶ τὸ κάτωθεν μέρος τῆς ἐνδιπλώσεως. καὶ γίνεται τοῦτο διά τε τὰς φυσικὰς τοῦ δέρματος τραχύτητας, καὶ μάλιστα ἐν τοῖς παρηβῶσι σώμασιν, καὶ ὅτι χαλαρόν ἐστι τοῖς ἄλλοις σχεδὸν ἅπασι, τοῖς τε ἰσχνοῖς καὶ πρεσβύταις μάλιστα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παραπτομέναις
For we all clearly perceive the very opposite: rubbing with 508 oil is even, while dry rubbing is uneven. For in dry rubbings the hands drag the skin along with them and, as it were, double it and fold it over upon itself, firmly and contrary to nature. Then, when the former fold, so to speak, can no longer follow the hands as they pass over it, this fold runs back and is drawn back toward the continuous part, while the hands pass all at once to another part of the skin, skipping over the under part of the fold; and for this reason we perceive such rubbing as hard, uneven, and fatiguing: hard, because the skin is forcibly stretched and bruised, and for the same reasons also fatiguing; uneven, because the rubbing hands do not fall upon every part of the skin, but always skip over the under part of the fold. And this happens both because of the natural roughnesses of the skin, especially in bodies past their prime, and because it is slack in almost all other people, and most of all in the lean and in old men.

For we all clearly perceive the very opposite: rubbing with 508 oil is even, and dry rubbing is uneven. For in dry rubbings the hands draw the skin along with them and, as it were, double it and fold it over upon itself, firmly and contrary to nature. Then, when the earlier doubling, as one may call it, can no longer follow the hands as they glide along past it, this doubling runs back and is drawn back to the continuous skin, while the hands pass all at once to another part of the skin, passing over the lower part of the doubling. For this reason we perceive such rubbing as hard, uneven and fatiguing: hard because the skin is forcibly stretched and bruised, and fatiguing for the same reasons; uneven because the rubbing hands do not fall upon every part of the skin but always pass over the lower part of the doubling. This happens both because of the natural roughnesses of the skin, especially in bodies past their prime, and because the skin is slack in almost all others, most of all in the lean and the old.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For if the skin were smooth and uniform throughout and precisely stretched, it would not be folded over 509 easily, nor would it make the movements of the hands over it uneven; rather, I think, they would run easily forward, slipping readily from the first parts to others, because they would drag along with themselves no part of the skin. But since it is slack and rough, it needs some drug from outside that will fill up its wrinkles and supply slipperiness to the rubbing hands; and for these purposes you would find nothing more suitable than oil. And one might wonder at Archidamus, who neither showed respect for what is evidently apparent nor discovered the account of it, but declared the opposite on both points. For dry rubbing appears uneven, and that with oil even, and reason bears witness to these things not least by explaining the cause. But concerning Archidamus and Diocles, enough for the present purposes. For I wish, since I have once come to demonstrating the faulty road of the discovery of the powers, using oil as an example, to go through the whole argument as quickly as possible.

K11.509-510K. XI 509–510, ch. 2.20 difficulty low (0.69/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
λεγόντων οὑν τῶν φιλοσόφων θερμὸν εἶναι 510 τοὔλαιον καὶ τεκμήριον τούτου τιθεμένων οὐ σμικρὸν, ὡς οἴονται, τὸ τάχος τῆς εἰς τὴν φλόγα μεταβολῆς ἀντιλέγοντες ἔνιοι τουτὶ μὲν ὑπάρχειν αὐτῷ φασιν, ὅτι λεπτόν τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ ἀερῶδες. ἐκ δὲ τοῦ πήγνυσθαι ταχέως ἐν τῷ χειμῶνι σαφῶς ἐνδείκνυσθαι τὴν ψύξιν. οὐ μόνον γὰρ οἴνου καὶ ὄξους, ἀλλὰ καὶ αὐτοῦ τοῦ ὕδατος ἑτοιμότερον ὑπὸ τοῦ ψυχροῦ νικᾶσθαι. πρὸς ταῦτα δὲ ἀντιλέγοντες ἕτεροι, τὸ μὲν ἀερῶδες ὑπάρχειν αὐτὸ συγχωροῦσιν καὶ φαίνεσθαί γε σαφῶς τοῦτό φασιν ἐκ τῆς κουφότητος, οὐ μὴν καὶ ψυχρόν γε εἶναι τὴν δύναμιν ὁμολογοῦσιν. ἀέρα γὰρ ἅπαντα θερμὸν ὑπάρχειν τὴν φύσιν, ὡς δηλοῖ καὶ ἡ γένεσις αὐτοῦ. λεπτυνθὲν οὖν καὶ λυθὲν ὑπὸ θερμότητος ὕδωρ, ἀέρα γίγνεσθαι. καὶ δῆλον ὡς ἐντεῦθεν αὐτοὺς διαδέχεται μέγιστον ζήτημα, τὸ καὶ τοῖς ἐνδοξοτάτοις φιλοσόφοις ἠμφισβητημένον, Ἀριστοτέλους μὲν καὶ τῶν ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ θερμὸν ὑπολαμβανόντων εἶναι τὸν ἀέρα, τῶν δ’ ἀπὸ τῆς στοᾶς ψυχρόν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
So when the philosophers say that olive oil is 510 hot, and set down as no small evidence of this, as they think, the speed of its change into flame, some people object and say that this indeed belongs to it because it is thin and airy, but that from its congealing quickly in winter it clearly displays its coldness; for it is more readily overcome by the cold not only than wine and vinegar, but even than water itself. Others, objecting to these claims, concede that it is airy, and say that this appears clearly from its lightness, yet they do not also agree that it is cold in its power. For all air is hot in its nature, as its generation also shows: for water, having been thinned and dissolved by heat, becomes air. And it is clear that from this point a very great question takes them over, one disputed even among the most reputable philosophers, Aristotle and his followers supposing that air is hot, while those from the Stoa suppose it cold.

For all air, they say, is hot in its nature, as its generation also shows: water, when thinned and dissolved by heat, becomes air. And it is clear that from this point a very great question succeeds to them, one disputed even among the most renowned philosophers, Aristotle and those under him supposing air to be hot, and those from the Stoa supposing it to be cold.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But also concerning the oil's congealing quickly, which seemed to those who declare it cold [to be a proof], those who assert that it is hot counter-propose the fact that it does not congeal violently, as water does. 511 For they say that what happens in its case is not even congealing at all, if we examine the matter precisely, but a kind of setting and thickening, and they say that this comes about because it is thick and viscous, not because it is cold; so that here too the argument from either side again arrives at an impasse, the one party establishing opposite conclusions from the fact that oil is overcome by cooling sooner than water, the other from the fact that water congeals more than oil. In addition, then, to disputing about these matters, not even all agree that it is airy. For its lightness, they say, is a sign of this, but its thickness is no small evidence that it is earthy. And some, combining both, say that it is at once airy and earthy. There are also those who laugh at these people, on the grounds that by their account they completely remove what belongs most of all to oil, namely its moistness. In addition to these points, then, from its being viscous some demonstrate that it is cold, others that it is hot, and some that it is dry, others that it is moist.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅπερ ἐδόκει τοῖς ψυχρὸν ἀποφαίνουσιν, σύστασίν
But concerning oil's congealing quickly, too, which was the view of those who declare it cold, those who assert that it is hot counter-propose that it does not congeal vigorously, as water does. 511 For they say that what happens in its case is not even congelation at all, if we examine it precisely, but a sort of consolidation and thickening; and they say this occurs because it is thick and viscous, not because it is cold. So here too, once again, the argument arrives at an impasse from both sides: the one party establishes the contrary from the fact that oil is overcome by cooling more quickly than water, the other from the fact that water congeals more than oil. In addition, then, to the dispute on these points, not even all agree that it is airy. For its lightness, they say, is a token of this, but its thickness is no small proof that it is earthy. Some, combining both, say that it is at once airy and earthy. But there are those who laugh at these people, on the grounds that in their account they entirely take away moistness, which is what most belongs to oil. Furthermore, from its being viscous, some demonstrate that it is cold, some that it is hot, and some that it is dry, others that it is moist.

Greek (Kühn)
εἰ μὴ γὰρ συνῆκτο καὶ πεπύκνωτο τὰ μόρια καὶ ἀλλήλων ἀκριβῶς εἴχετο, μὴ ἂν γενέσθαι γλίσχρον, 512 ἔργα δ’ εἶναι ταῦτα σύμπαντα ψυχροῦ. διαχεῖν γὰρ καὶ λύειν καὶ λεπτύνειν καὶ ἀπ’ ἀλλήλων χωρίζειν τὰ μόρια τὴν τοῦ θερμοῦ φύσιν. οἱ δ’ αὖ τοὐναντίον οὐδ’ ἂν γενέσθαι τι γλίσχρον φασὶν ἄνευ θερμότητος κρατούσης, ὡς κᾀν ταῖς ἑψήσεσι δηλοῦσθαι τῶν χυλῶν ἁπάντων, ὅσοι τε βοτανῶν ἐκθλίβονται καὶ ὅσοι καρπῶν. ὑδατώδεις γὰρ ὄντας τὰ πρώτα καὶ λεπτοὺς ταῖς συστάσεσιν καὶ διαῤῥέοντας, ἐπειδὰν ἑψηθῶσιν, παχεῖς καὶ γλίσχρους γίνεσθαι. καὶ μὲν δὴ κᾀκ τῶν κατὰ μέρος παραδειγμάτων ἑκάτεροι τἀναντία πιθανῶς κατασκευάζουσιν, οἱ μὲν ἰξὸν καὶ μέλι καὶ πίτταν ὑγρὰν καὶ ῥητίνην, ἕτερά τε τοιαῦτα τῶν ὁμολογουμένων γλίσχρων τε ἅμα καὶ θερμῶν προτείνοντες. οἱ δὲ κωνείου καὶ ψυλλίου καὶ ἀνδραφάξυος καὶ βλίτου χυλοῦ μνημονεύοντες, ἔτι δὲ πρὸς τούτοις οἷον κολοφῶνά τινα τῷ λόγῳ τὸ φλέγμα προχειριζόμενοι· σαφῶς γὰρ αὐτοῖς τοῦτο καὶ γλίσχρον εἶναι δοκεῖ καὶ ψυχρόν. ἀλλὰ κᾀκ τοῦ τοῖς φλεβοτομουμένοις ἐπισταζόμενον ἔλαιον εὔρουν ἐργάζεσθαι τὸ αἷμα τἀναντία κατασκευάζοντάς ἐστιν ἰδεῖν ἑκατέρους.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἰξὸν, κολοφῶνά
For if the parts had not been drawn together and condensed and held closely to one another, it would not have become viscous; 512 and these are all works of the cold. For it is the nature of the hot to diffuse and loosen and thin the parts and to separate them from one another. Others, conversely, say that nothing would become viscous without heat prevailing, as is shown also in the boiling of all juices, both those that are pressed out of herbs and those from fruits. For being at first watery and thin in their consistency and running, once they have been boiled they become thick and viscous. And indeed from particular examples too each side plausibly establishes the opposite conclusions: the one proposing bird-lime and honey and liquid pitch and resin and other such things among those agreed to be at once both viscous and hot; the other mentioning the juice of hemlock and fleawort and orach and blite, and in addition to these bringing forward phlegm as a kind of colophon to their argument, for this clearly seems to them to be both viscous and cold. But also from the fact that oil dripped onto those who are being phlebotomized makes the blood flow freely one can see each side establishing opposite conclusions.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διαῤῥέοντας, κολοφῶνά, ἐπισταζόμενον
For if its parts had not been drawn together and condensed and did not hold tightly to one another, it would not have become viscous; 512 and all these, they say, are works of the cold. For it is the nature of the hot to diffuse, loosen, thin, and separate the parts from one another. Others, on the contrary, say that nothing at all would become viscous without heat prevailing, as is shown also in the boiling of all juices, both those pressed out of herbs and those pressed out of fruits. For being watery at first, thin in their consistency and running freely, once they are boiled they become thick and viscous. And indeed, from particular examples as well, each side plausibly establishes the opposite conclusion: the one side putting forward birdlime, honey, liquid pitch and resin, and other such things that are agreed to be at once viscous and hot; the other side mentioning the juice of hemlock, fleawort, orache and blite, and, in addition to these, bringing forward phlegm as a kind of finishing stroke to the argument; for this seems to them plainly to be both viscous and cold. But also from the fact that oil dripped onto those being phlebotomised makes the blood flow freely, one can see each side establishing opposite conclusions.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φιλολογίας, ἄλης
For the one party say that it produces the free flow by diffusing [the blood] through its heat, the other that it does so by furnishing slipperiness through its coldness, 513 like ice. And then, turning aside from this point, they discourse about the nature of all slippery things, ascending in their argument to the furthest extremity of learned disquisition. But others, condemning the wandering of these men, as they call it, bring forward soft fat for the argument, which is closest to olive oil in its nature but can be clearly discovered as to what sort it is in its mixture; for since it is formed within ourselves it could not escape notice. To begin with, at any rate, they say that in healthy and well-nourished bodies much soft fat is generated, bodies which no one, not even if mad, would call cold; for such bodies come about when nature is strong, and nature all the natural philosophers have agreed to be a hot thing. But some, contradicting these, say that soft fat is generated in colder mixtures, as women and those who live an idle life show.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χέον, φιλολογίας, ἄλης, ὥς φασιν
For the one party say that it produces the free flow as liquefying [the blood] by its heat, the others as 513 furnishing slipperiness by its coldness, in the manner of ice. And then, turning aside from this point, they hold discourse about the nature of all slippery things, ascending in their argument to the very extremity of learned disputation. Others, condemning the wandering of these men, as they say, put forward fat for the argument, since it is very close to oil in nature, yet can be clearly discovered as to what sort it is in its mixture; for since it is formed within us ourselves, it could not escape notice. For instance, they say that much fat is generated in healthy and well-nourished bodies, which not even a madman would call cold. For such things come about when nature is strong, and nature all the natural philosophers have agreed to be a hot thing. But some, contradicting these, say that fat is generated in colder mixtures, as women and those who live idly make plain.

K11.513-514K. XI 513–514, ch. 2.20 difficulty mid (1.56/100; thin 3, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ἤδη δὲ καὶ τῶν ἐθνῶν, ἔνθα μὲν τὸ ψυχρὸν κρατεῖ, πιμελώδεις, ἔνθα δὲ τὸ θερμὸν, ἰσχνούς φασι γίνεσθαι τοὺς ἀνθρώπους καὶ προχειρίζονται παράδειγμα τῷ λόγῳ, Κελτοὺς μὲν καὶ Θρᾷκας καὶ ﻿ 514 Βιθυνοὺς καὶ Ποντικοὺς καὶ Γαλάτας, ἅμα μὲν ψυχρᾶς χώρας οἰκήτορας, εὐθὺς δὲ καὶ πιμελώδεις τοὐπίπαν· Ἄραβας δὲ καὶ Λίβυας Αἰγυπτίους τε καὶ Αἰθίοπας, ὅσοι τ’ ἄλλοι θερμὴν ἐποικοῦσι γῆν ἰσχνοὺς καὶ ξηροὺς ἅπαντας. εἰσὶ δ’ οἳ καὶ χωρὶς κατὰ μέρος παραδειγμάτων αὐτῷ τῷ καθόλου λόγῳ πρὸς ἀλλήλους ἀμφισβητοῦσιν, οἱ μὲν μηδ’ ἂν γενέσθαι τὸν λιπαρὸν ἐν ἡμῖν χυμὸν, εἰ μὴ καλῶς ὑπὸ τῆς φύσεως αἱ τροφαὶ κατεργασθεῖεν, οἱ δὲ τοῦτον μὲν ἐν ταῖς τροφαῖς περιέχεσθαι, καταναλίσκεσθαι δ’ ὑπὸ τῆς ἐν ἡμῖν θερμασίας, ὥσπερ τοὔλαιον ὑπὸ τῆς φλογὸς, ἧς ἀῤῥώστου γινομένης ἀθροίζεσθαι κατὰ τὸ σῶμα. τινὲς δὲ αὐτῶν καὶ τὰ ζῶα παρατίθενται τῷ λόγῳ, τὰ μὲν φύσει ψυχρότερα πιμελωδέστερα πάντα ὑπάρχοντα, καθάπερ ἄρκτοι καὶ ὄϊες καὶ σύες, τὰ δ’ αὖ θερμότερα ξηρότερα καὶ ἀπίμελα, καθάπερ λέοντές τε καὶ παρδάλεις καὶ κύνες. ἔτι τοίνυν ἀπὸ τοῦ διαφθείρεσθαι βρεχομένας ἐλαίῳ μελίττας τε καὶ μυίας καὶ σφῆκας καὶ μύρμηκας, οἱ μὲν ψυχρὸν, οἱ δὲ θερμὸν ἐπιδεικνύναι πειρῶνται τοὔλαιον, καὶ τινὲς μὲν ὑγρὸν, τινὲς δὲ ξηρόν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And further, among the nations too, they say that where cold prevails men become fatty, and where heat prevails, lean; and they put forward as an example for their argument the Celts, Thracians, 514 Bithynians, Pontics and Galatians, who are at once inhabitants of a cold country and straightway also fatty for the most part; whereas the Arabs, Libyans, Egyptians and Ethiopians, and all others who dwell in a hot land, are all lean and dry. There are also some who, even apart from particular examples, dispute with one another by means of the general argument itself: the one side holding that the oily humour would not even come to be in us unless the nutriments were well worked up by nature, the other that this humour is contained in the nutriments but is consumed by the warmth in us, just as oil is by the flame, and that when this warmth becomes weak it is accumulated throughout the body. Some of them also adduce the animals for the argument, those which are colder by nature being all fattier, such as bears, sheep and pigs, and those in turn which are hotter being drier and without fat, such as lions, leopards and dogs. Further, then, from the fact that bees, flies, wasps and ants are destroyed when wetted with oil, some try to demonstrate that oil is cold, others that it is hot, and some that it is moist, others that it is dry.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
Further, regarding peoples, they say that where cold prevails men become fat, but where heat prevails, lean; and they put forward as an example for the argument the Celts, Thracians, 514 Bithynians, Pontians and Galatians, who are at once inhabitants of a cold country and also, as a rule, fat; and the Arabs, Libyans, Egyptians and Ethiopians, and all others who inhabit a hot land, all lean and dry. There are also those who, even apart from particular examples, dispute with one another over the general argument itself: some say that the oily humour would not even come into being in us unless the foods were properly worked up by nature; others say that it is contained in the foods, but is consumed by the warmth in us, as oil is by the flame, and that when this warmth becomes weak, it collects in the body. Some of them also adduce animals for the argument: those that are colder by nature are all fattier, such as bears, sheep and pigs, whereas those that are hotter are drier and without fat, such as lions, leopards and dogs. Moreover, from the fact that bees, flies, wasps and ants are destroyed when wetted with oil, some try to show that oil is cold, others that it is hot, and some that it is moist, others that it is dry.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For since the animals mentioned are bloodless and for this reason cold, 515 some say that they are readily chilled by the oil and die on that account; others, that, being thoroughly heated, they do not endure the change to the opposite. And further, others say that they are dried immoderately, because they have already been dried out by nature, and are harmed by reason of the excess; while yet others say that they are moistened, by the contrariety to their nature. Now that they should argue in opposite directions from one another in the case of the insects would perhaps be nothing to wonder at; but that they also argue for either side from what happens to us ourselves is indeed worthy of wonder. For some say that one sweats more, others less, if one anoints oneself while bathing; and neither do those who say 'more' agree with one another about the general power, nor those who say 'less'.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And the same things are said also 517 about the fact that earth is not properly soaked by oil, although it is wetted by the other liquids, such as water, wine and vinegar. Here too some blame the dryness of its substance, some its fine-particled nature, and there are those who blame its coarse-particled nature: those who blame the fineness of parts say that it passes through the pores of the earth without touching them, while those who blame the coarseness of parts say that it does not enter them at all. And it is not possible to refute either party, since on either cause certain things can also fail to mix with what is adjacent to them, if indeed to some of the natural philosophers too the commensurability of the pores seems to be the cause of mixture. I for my part am now going through these matters under heads, recalling only the starting-points of the arguments. But if someone wished to extend each of them and to establish it from both sides, in that way he would, I think, fill not one book but very many. And it is already possible to observe that such wandering of the arguments is not only long—for that would be tolerable—but also ascends to the summit of natural philosophy, and sometimes is also hard to decide.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἅτι, καὶ ὡς διορίζεσθαι
But if one should also know that it is altogether 518 useless to physicians, one will, I think, avoid such a path of arguments still more. For we inquire neither simply whether each of the drugs is hot, or cold, or dry, or moist, nor whether it is moistening, or drying, or heating, or cooling for ants, or wasps, or bees, or any other of the animals, but how it stands in relation to the human body. Since, then, it is possible to make the test on human beings, they do not do well to turn aside to other things. The correct test is first that on well-tempered bodies, next that on ill-tempered ones, and then, as has often been said by us before as well, that on hot and cold and dry and moist diseases. In these we required that one pay attention, and also that one distinguish that which acts in itself from that which acts incidentally.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐγκαύσεσιν, ψυγμοῖς
And this will come about, first, if it is applied to affections that are as simple as possible; second, if it displays its own power perceptibly right from the beginning, or after a time, or even once it has been completely removed, as cold water does which produces a recall of warmth 519; and further, in addition to these, if it is unmixed with any other quality, insofar as it is possible to produce such a thing. How, then, one might produce oil of this kind has been stated before; but how one might apply it to a simple disease will now be stated, after we have first recalled this much, that, as was shown in the treatise on the differences of diseases, some bodies have been turned toward the hotter without any cacochymia or inflammation of the chest or of a viscus, as is wont to happen in severe cases of heat-stroke (ἐγκαύσεις), while others have been turned toward the colder, as in what are now customarily called by all people 'chills' (ψυγμοί). In such conditions, then, by applying oil you will discover clearly whether it is by nature such as to heat us or rather to cool. Let it, obviously, be free of every strong acquired quality; for one must always bear this in mind in the case of every drug. This, then, is the main point of the account; but the actual outcome is shown by experience. Those who have suffered heat-stroke, in addition to not ceasing from the heat, become also more nauseous and, as time goes on, hotter.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: Μηδείας
520 But for those who have been chilled, clearly nothing results from anointing with oil, whether toward benefit or harm. From which it is also clear that, even if it is by nature such as to heat us, yet certainly it is not so vehemently or manifestly, as resin and pitch and asphalt are. And you must understand now neither dry resin or pitch or asphalt—for no one would even anoint himself with these—nor indeed those melted in the sun, for such a thing would no longer be a simple or naturally occurring drug; but, in the case of resin, either the one called 'self-flowing', or the terebinth and mastic resin while still liquid and fresh, or, of the pine resin, the oil-like part that stands on top in the jars; and, in the case of pitch, the liquid kind, and still more the oil-like part floating upon it, which indeed they call pissanthos, for it really is like a sort of flower of pitch. And likewise asphalt, the liquid kind, and further that from Media.

520 But for those who have been chilled, no benefit or harm clearly results from anointing with oil. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄσης, λελευκασμένον, αὐξάνειν
For all these are oily and have a very great share of the oil-like nature, yet they heat manifestly, just as stacte myrrh and pine oil, or juniper oil, or cedar and laurel oil, and other such things, clearly heat through those who have been chilled, 521 but in the extreme distress those who have been burned by heat. These, then, are agreed from both sides to heat. But oil is agreed from neither side either strongly to heat through those who have been chilled or to harm those who have been burned by heat. Yet it is not obscurely, either, that it becomes a cause of a certain nausea (ἄση) for those who have fallen into fever as a result of heat-burning (ἔγκαυσις), and it appears to augment the heat, just as also in pure erysipelas. These are surely bilious fluxes; for surely in these very cases too it is clearly seen to increase the warmth, even if you apply it extremely cold. For in cases of heat-burning, if it be without salt, and in addition whitened, it affords some relief when poured over cold. But in cases of erysipelas, however cold it may be, it benefits nothing, but is even of a nature to harm: those that arise purely from a bilious flux, all of them, with the greatest harms, and those from blood mixed with it, with smaller ones, though not even these are trivial. And less than these, but still manifestly, it also distresses seething inflammations; from all of which, as I inferred, it seemed to me to kindle a heat closely similar to that of whatever bodies it happens to have come into contact with.

For all these are oily and have very much of the oleaginous nature, yet they heat manifestly; just as stacte myrrh, and pine oil, or juniper oil, or cedar oil and laurel oil, and other such things, clearly heat thoroughly those who have been chilled, 521 but distress to the utmost those who have been scorched. For it could not benefit, because it does not heat strongly, nor was it capable of harming, since it does not cool at all; whereas, once it had taken on a strong acquired heat, it benefited sufficiently—whenever we set those so affected into tubs 523 of hot oil containing certain roots, or heating herbs, boiled down in it. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὸ μέν τι ... τὸ δέ τι (elliptical construction, verb supplied)
The sources of this kind of dispute are three: the failure to distinguish the kinds of things that are said to heat 524 us, the confused use of homonymies, and the failure to leave room for any mean. Now things are said to heat us both when they are hot in actuality, like fire itself, and when hot in potentiality; and of the things hot in potentiality, some do so as foods, others as drugs, others as coverings. And as for 'the hot' itself, we are accustomed to name thus both what is already such in actuality and what is so in potentiality, as has often been said before as well. But that which is hot in actuality includes both what is hot simply, like fire, and what is hot by predominance, like the animal, and what is hot when compared with the well-proportioned member of the same genus, and what is hot relative to anything whatever that happens to be at hand. What is hot in potentiality, on the other hand, is first distinguished by opposition from what is hot accidentally, and then in turn has as many differences of signification as what is hot in actuality has. For being, as it were, a kind of matter suitable for what is hot in actuality, one part of it is suited to the things called hot simply, another to those hot by predominance; and another would be the proper matter for things compared with the well-proportioned member of the same genus or of the same species, or with anything whatever that happens to be at hand.

The hot in potentiality is, first, distinguished in opposition to the hot by accident, and then, next, it has as many differences of meaning as the hot in actuality has. Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But, hastening to put an end to the present 526 discourse, I return once more to olive oil. For it happens to it, as a nutriment, perhaps to heat us, whenever it is taken into the interior of the body; for it seems to certain physicians and philosophers of no mean repute that, just as from foods, so also from olive oil there comes about a certain addition to the body. And it also happens to it, as a drug, to be in some way intermediate between the things that heat and those that cool us; and it belongs to it, moreover, as a covering, to warm us—and this, indeed, seems to me to be the most proper likeness, above all others, of its nature. For just as each of our garments, being warmed by us ourselves, is naturally disposed to warm us in return, so too is olive oil. But just as none of those becomes hotter than the very body that warms it, so neither does olive oil.

Hastening to bring the present 526 discussion to an end, I return once more to olive oil. For it belongs to it, as a nutriment, perhaps to heat us when it is taken into the interior of the body; for it seems to some physicians and philosophers of no mean repute that an addition to the body comes about from oil, just as it does from foods. It belongs to it also, as a drug, to be in a way intermediate between the things that heat us and those that cool us. And it belongs to it as well to heat us as a covering, and indeed the most proper image of its nature seems to me to be something of this kind. For just as each of our garments, being heated by us ourselves, naturally heats us in return, so too does oil. But just as none of those garments becomes hotter than the very body that heats it, so neither does oil.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀφιεσμάτων
The whole matter would be well understood by you — and it will prove useful not only for the present but also for everything that is to come — if you were to call to mind the very air that surrounds us, which sometimes becomes colder than the heat within us, to such a degree that we need certain screens and coverings against it, and at other times becomes hotter again, to such a degree that we cannot bear it but flee for refuge into 527 some shelter and shade. And there are times when it is in such a moderate state that we desire neither the things that would warm us nor the things that cool us, but our contact with it is painless and imperceptible. This third mixture, then, the one just described, I call moderate, and I say that it is intermediate between the others and neither heats our body nor cools it; of the other two, the one heats us and the other cools us. And I say that both the warmth that comes from garments and the warmth that comes from oil resemble the intermediate and moderate mixture of the air that surrounds us. But if even so what I wish to indicate is not clear, I think you will understand it better in the following way. Conceive for me two human beings, similarly mixed in body, touching one another either with the chest and belly alone, or also with the hands and legs. If, then, the air surrounding them should have the same mixture as these two human beings, so that the warmth in the three bodies is equal, then in such a case nothing could be said to be heated by anything.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For bodies that are alike in every respect undergo 528 no alteration from one another. But if the surrounding air is cold, the men would be said to warm each other, although they are not in fact warming, but keeping off the cold. Accordingly the many, being accustomed to use names loosely, call that which prevents one from being cooled "warming", not correctly. Now one must allow them to name things as they wish, but that the same thing is signified by the verb "to warm" in each of the two expressions must in no way be further allowed. For it is certainly not the same thing to supply warmth to something from oneself, or to keep off the cold. This being so, whatever things fall upon us from outside and have the same warmth as the body, whether they have taken it from themselves or from other things or indeed from ourselves, these, according to the strict account, must be said neither to warm us nor to cool us; but if anyone wishes to use the term loosely, it must be allowed, provided only that in the expression he recalls the difference of the meanings. For it is in one way that the warmer air is said to warm us, and in another way the well-tempered air.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔτι, εἰληφότα, ἀναμιμνήσκοντα
For bodies alike in every respect, at one another's hands, 528 undergo no alteration. But if the surrounding air is cold, the men would be said to warm one another, although they are not in fact warming but keeping off the cold. The many, then, being accustomed to misuse names, call what prevents being chilled 'warming', and not correctly. We must, then, allow them to name things as they wish; but we must by no means go on to allow them to signify the same thing by the verb 'to warm' in each of the two expressions. For it is surely not the same thing to supply warmth to something from oneself and to prevent the cold. This being so, whatever things fall upon us from outside having the same warmth as the body, whether they have taken it from themselves, or from others, or even from us ourselves, these must, by the strict account, be said neither to warm us nor to cool us. But if anyone wishes to use the terms loosely, it must be allowed, provided only that in the expression he keeps in mind the difference between the meanings. For the warmer air is said to warm us in one way, and the moderate air in another.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προβλήματος, μεμαθηκότες;
Analogous to the hotter [air] are pellitory and mustard and 529 pepper, and to the moderate [air] soft fat and oil. For the airs are hot in actuality, the drugs in potentiality. For we ourselves do not make the air hot, but [we are heated] because we come close to air of such a kind; whereas the drugs, as we showed also in the third book On Mixtures, taking from us the beginning of the change toward being heated, are of such a nature as to heat us back in return, some to an equal degree, some more, some less. Now oil belongs among those that heat back equally. And it also belongs to it, from its being viscous and hard to run off and obstructive of the pores, to augment the warmth, as it seems, in a twofold way: on the one hand from its being plastered around in the manner of a garment or a screen, and on the other, from its blocking up the pores, the preventing of the greater part of the vaporous heat from flowing out from within. For these reasons, then, it does not even allow our bodies to be dried, unless it is applied warm and over a longer time; and all men, having learned this power of it by experience —
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς δοκεῖν, μεμαθηκότες; (the question mark seems an error: the sentence continues into the next passage), ἐκ δὲ τοῦ τοὺς πόρους ἐμφράττειν τὸ κωλύειν (the construction is loose)
Pellitory, mustard and 529 pepper stand in a relation analogous to the hotter air, and fat and oil to the moderate air. For the airs are hot in actuality, but the drugs are hot in potentiality. We ourselves do not make the air hot; rather, we are affected because we come close to air of that kind. But the drugs, as I also showed in the third book On Mixtures, take from us the starting-point of their change towards being heated. Some of them are then naturally disposed to heat us in return to an equal degree, others more, others less. Among those that heat in return to an equal degree is oil. Because it is viscous, hard to flow off, and obstructive of the pores, it also has the property of increasing, in a twofold way, so it seems, the warmth. By being plastered round the body like a garment or a protective screen, and by obstructing the pores, it prevents the greater part of the vaporous heat from flowing away from within. For these reasons, then, it does not allow our bodies to be dried either, unless it is applied hot and for a longer time. And all men, having learned this power of it by experience—

those whom they wish to sweat more abundantly, whether bathed or unbathed, they do not apply oil to them straight away at the beginning, 530 but in the case of those who have been ill, once they have begun to sweat, they warm it and pour it over them; in the case of those who are ill, once they have sweated sufficiently; and in the case of the healthy, those who come from a long journey on foot and from rather many exercises, or who have had their body dried out in any other way whatsoever, these they allow to anoint themselves even before sweating; but those who have failed to digest, or who have a plethoric body, these they do not permit to use oil even when they bathe. And it seems to me that here too oil is in the middle between the things that moisten us and those that dry us, just as a little earlier it was shown to be between those that heat and those that cool. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἱδρῶντες, συντήγματα
At any rate, olive oil applied simply, just as it is, without having been warmed beforehand by ourselves through artifice, or cooled — for this one must always bear in mind in every case — preserves our mixture such as it found it, restraining as far as possible and preventing all the vaporous transpirations from being evacuated from the body. But if you rub the 531 limbs with oil for a longer time, you will both generate a certain acquired heat from the rubbing, and you will benefit those suffering from fatigue in this way: partly because the fat penetrates inside the body into the fine pores so as to soften what has been hardened, partly because by its heat it disperses whatever pungent and thin moisture, generated in fatigues from colliquation, was being held in beneath the skin. For sweats are seen flowing off outwardly in those who exercise, and not a few residues and, as it were, colliquative products are seen flowing together inwardly into the belly. And from these one must reckon that, even if it is not evident, many such things in need of evacuation are also left behind throughout the whole body. For in fatigues a twofold condition arises, as has been demonstrated elsewhere: the homoeomerous parts of the animal are dried, while the empty spaces are moistened, not with a useful moisture in accordance with nature — for that would be plethora — but with one that is thin, pungent and biting; and for this reason they also require remedies of two kinds, those that moisten the dryness and those that evacuate the pungent moisture.

So, at any rate, oil applied simply in this way, without being warmed beforehand or cooled by us ourselves through artificial treatment (for this must always be borne in mind in every case), preserves our mixture just as it received it, shutting in as far as possible and preventing all the vaporous transpirations from being evacuated from the body. But if you rub the 531 limbs with oil at greater length, you will generate a certain acquired heat from the rubbing, and in this you will benefit those who are fatigued: on the one hand because the fat sinks inward into the body, into the fine pores, so as to soften what has become hardened; on the other because by its heat it disperses whatever pungent and thin fluid, generated in fatigues from colliquation, was being held in by the skin. For sweats are seen flowing off outwardly in those who exercise, and not a few residues are seen flowing together inwardly into the belly, as it were colliquated matter. From these things one must reason, even if it is not apparent, that many such things requiring evacuation are also left behind throughout the whole body. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χυτλοῦσθαι, ξηραλειφεῖν, ἀνακόπτηται
And for this reason, against both of these, the baths in 532 sweet waters happen to be most useful, since by their moisture they correct the dryness of the organs, and by their heat in turn they also disperse the thin humour. And if indeed water were able to remain in bodies and not easily flow away, it alone would be sufficient. But since what is poured over from outside flows off easily, and what enters into the pores of the body flows out again, having no hold, for this very purpose oil is usefully mixed in, and the more so if one is rubbed down at length with both together, which the ancients called chytlousthai, and they set over against it xēraleiphein (dry anointing). This too is in some way, in and of itself, curative of fatigues, but it falls very far short of chytlōsasthai. For, apart from everything else, whenever the water is beaten up and rubbed together with oil, it has a mixture that is quite hard to dissolve, so that on whatever bodies the oil is smeared and plastered around, the water too is compelled to remain in contact with these for a longer time. So here too, then, oil is as it were a kind of material of moistening and drying agents, just as it was shown earlier to be of heating and cooling ones.
